# Daily Summary: 2025-04-09

### BRIDGES: Bridging Graph Modality and Large Language Models within EDA Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05180v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Li, Yang Zou, Christopher Ellis, Ruben Purdy, Shawn Blanton, José M. F. Moura
- **Abstract**: While many EDA tasks already involve graph-based data, existing LLMs in EDA primarily either represent graphs as sequential text, or simply ignore graph-structured data that might be beneficial like dataflow graphs of RTL code. Recent studies have found that LLM performance suffers when graphs are represented as sequential text, and using additional graph information significantly boosts performance. To address these challenges, we introduce BRIDGES, a framework designed to incorporate graph modality into LLMs for EDA tasks. BRIDGES integrates an automated data generation workflow, a solution that combines graph modality with LLM, and a comprehensive evaluation suite. First, we establish an LLM-driven workflow to generate RTL and netlist-level data, converting them into dataflow and netlist graphs with function descriptions. This workflow yields a large-scale dataset comprising over 500,000 graph instances and more than 1.5 billion tokens. Second, we propose a lightweight cross-modal projector that encodes graph representations into text-compatible prompts, enabling LLMs to effectively utilize graph data without architectural modifications. Experimental results demonstrate 2x to 10x improvements across multiple tasks compared to text-only baselines, including accuracy in design retrieval, type prediction and perplexity in function description, with negligible computational overhead (<1% model weights increase and <30% additional runtime overhead). Even without additional LLM finetuning, our results outperform text-only by a large margin. We plan to release BRIDGES, including the dataset, models, and training flow.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "BRIDGES: Bridging Graph Modality and Large Language Models within EDA Tasks".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BRIDGES, a framework that integrates graph modality into Large Language Models (LLMs) for Electronic Design Automation (EDA) tasks. The framework addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based EDA approaches that primarily rely on text-based representations of designs, arguing that graph-structured data, like dataflow graphs, can significantly improve LLM performance. BRIDGES includes: 1) an automated data generation workflow to create a large-scale EDA-specific graph dataset; 2) a lightweight cross-modal projector to encode graph representations into text-compatible prompts; and 3) a comprehensive evaluation suite. The authors demonstrate that BRIDGES achieves substantial improvements in various EDA tasks, including design retrieval, type prediction, function description, and power/area estimation, compared to text-only baselines. The paper highlights the benefits of incorporating graph information and the effectiveness of the proposed framework without requiring extensive LLM fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating graph modality into LLMs for EDA tasks is not entirely new (GraphCodeBERT being a prominent example). However, BRIDGES distinguishes itself by focusing specifically on EDA, creating a large-scale EDA-specific dataset, and proposing a lightweight cross-modal projector. The novelty lies in the specific design choices and the application to the EDA domain. Graph modality itself is not a new idea.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the performance of LLMs in EDA tasks, which are inherently graph-structured. The creation of a large-scale, open-source EDA graph dataset is a valuable contribution to the community. Demonstrating significant performance gains across multiple EDA tasks, particularly with a relatively simple cross-modal projector, makes the paper impactful. However, the limited model sizes used in the experiments raises questions about the scalability of the method with the more powerful LLMs widely used today. The runtime increase might become more significant with larger datasets.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset:** The generation and release of a large-scale EDA-specific graph dataset address a significant gap in the field.
    *   **Framework Design:** The lightweight cross-modal projector is a practical and efficient way to integrate graph information into LLMs without requiring architectural modifications.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The thorough evaluation across multiple EDA tasks provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of BRIDGES.
    *   **Open-Source Commitment:** The commitment to open-source the framework, dataset, and models will facilitate further research and adoption.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Graph Encoder:** While the paper acknowledges that the graph encoder is not the primary focus, its choice of NetlistGNN raises questions about its suitability for all EDA graph types. A more flexible graph encoder architecture might be beneficial. The evaluation is also limited to netlist graphs.
    *   **Limited LLM Scale:** The experiments primarily used smaller LLMs (3B, 8B). Evaluating BRIDGES with larger, state-of-the-art LLMs would provide a more convincing demonstration of its scalability and effectiveness.
    *   **Runtime Overhead:** The 30% additional runtime overhead, though claimed negligible, could become a significant factor for complex EDA workflows, especially with larger models and datasets.
    *   **Ablation studies:** While ablation studies are performed, more in depth analysis into the exact reasons behind the performance gains would increase the impact.
    *   **Graph Encoder comparison:** Limited comparison of various graph encoders and the impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** BRIDGES has the potential to influence future research in LLM-based EDA by:
    *   Encouraging the development of more sophisticated graph encoders for EDA.
    *   Providing a benchmark dataset and evaluation suite for assessing graph-aware LLMs in EDA.
    *   Facilitating the integration of graph modality into various EDA tasks, leading to more efficient and accurate design automation.
    *   The released dataset allows for more LLM approaches to be developed.

**Justification for Score:**

While BRIDGES presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM-based EDA, its novelty is incremental, and its evaluation is limited by the scale of the LLMs used. The focus on EDA-specific data and the lightweight projector are strengths. However, questions remain about the scalability of the method and the generalizability of the graph encoder. The creation of an EDA specific graph data set is very useful. Taking all these factors into account, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Concise Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05185v1)
- **Authors**: Mehdi Fatemi, Banafsheh Rafiee, Mingjie Tang, Kartik Talamadupula
- **Abstract**: Despite significant advancements in large language models (LLMs), a major drawback of reasoning models is their enormous token usage, which increases computational cost, resource requirements, and response time. In this work, we revisit the core principles of reinforcement learning (RL) and, through mathematical analysis, demonstrate that the tendency to generate lengthy responses arises inherently from RL-based optimization during training. This finding questions the prevailing assumption that longer responses inherently improve reasoning accuracy. Instead, we uncover a natural correlation between conciseness and accuracy that has been largely overlooked. Moreover, we show that introducing a secondary phase of RL post-training, using a small set of problems and limited resources, can significantly reduce a model's chain of thought while maintaining or even enhancing accuracy. Finally, we validate our conclusions through extensive experimental results.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Concise Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning" and will provide a summary, a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing concern of excessive token usage in large language models (LLMs), particularly in reasoning tasks. It argues that the tendency for LLMs to generate lengthy "chain of thought" (CoT) responses during reinforcement learning (RL) post-training is not inherently necessary for improved accuracy but rather a consequence of the RL optimization process itself. The authors mathematically analyze the proximal policy optimization (PPO) loss function, demonstrating that incorrect answers tend to encourage longer responses while correct answers promote brevity.  They propose a two-phase RL training strategy: first, train on challenging problems to improve reasoning capabilities, and second, train on occasionally solvable problems to enforce conciseness.  The paper demonstrates experimentally that this two-phase approach can significantly reduce response length while maintaining or even improving accuracy, even with very limited training data. The results show that the models become more robust in terms of temperature settings as well.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core argument that length isn't strictly correlated with accuracy in the specific context of RL fine-tuning is partially novel. While previous works have hinted at diminishing returns or even negative impacts of extremely long responses, the paper takes a more fundamental stance, arguing that length increases are a *side effect* of the RL optimization and not the *cause* of accuracy improvements.
    *   The mathematical analysis of the PPO loss function and how it drives response length based on reward signal is a significant and novel contribution. It provides a formal explanation for the observed behavior.
    *   The two-phase RL training strategy is also novel in its explicit focus on first improving problem-solving capabilities and then separately enforcing conciseness.
    *   The exploration of extremely small datasets for RL fine-tuning is interesting.

*   **Significance:**

    *   If the findings hold true at scale, it has significant implications for the efficiency and cost-effectiveness of LLMs. Reducing token usage directly translates to lower computational costs, faster response times, and reduced resource requirements.
    *   The proposed two-phase training strategy offers a practical approach to optimizing LLMs for both accuracy and conciseness. This could influence how models are trained, potentially leading to more efficient and effective reasoning models.
    *   The demonstration that RL fine-tuning can be effective with very limited data challenges the current trend of scaling up training data and resources. This makes RL-based optimization more accessible to resource-constrained environments.
    *   The analysis of PPO loss provides a deeper understanding of RL training and could be extended to other RL algorithms.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a solid mathematical foundation for its claims, offering a compelling explanation for the observed phenomena.
    *   **Well-Designed Experiments:** The experimental setup is well-defined, and the results clearly support the proposed two-phase training strategy.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for training LLMs and could lead to more efficient and cost-effective models.
    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** The paper tackles the critical issue of token usage and efficiency in LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the experiments cover multiple datasets and models, further evaluation with more diverse tasks and larger-scale models would strengthen the findings. It has been shown with small models but it needs to be scaled to much bigger models.
    *   **Reliance on Specific RL Algorithm:** The analysis focuses primarily on PPO. It would be beneficial to investigate whether similar trends hold for other RL algorithms, although given that it is the dominant RL algorithm it is reasonable to base it here.
    *   **Generalizability:** The results should be more scrutinized on generalizability of small datasets. It is possible that more complex or varied tasks may benefit from higher datasets.
    *   **The concept of solvability is poorly defined**: The solvability is based on an average result of 64 runs with a given temperature. This might vary and influence the conclusion.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a compelling argument and provides strong evidence for its claims. The combination of theoretical analysis, experimental validation, and practical implications makes it a valuable contribution to the field. The potential impact of this work on improving the efficiency and cost-effectiveness of LLMs is significant. The weaknesses related to scope are acknowledged and can be future research. Considering all factors, the novelty and significance of this paper justify the following score:

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### P2Mark: Plug-and-play Parameter-intrinsic Watermarking for Neural Speech Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05197v1)
- **Authors**: Yong Ren, Jiangyan Yi, Tao Wang, Jianhua Tao, Zhengqi Wen, Chenxing Li, Zheng Lian, Ruibo Fu, Ye Bai, Xiaohui Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recently, a large number of advanced neural speech generation methods have emerged in the open-source community. Although this has facilitated the application and development of technology, it has also increased the difficulty of preventing the abuse of generated speech and protecting copyrights. Audio watermarking technology is an effective method for proactively protecting generated speech, but when the source codes and model weights of the neural speech generation methods are open-sourced, audio watermarks based on previous watermarking methods can be easily removed or manipulated. This paper proposes a Plug-and-play Parameter-intrinsic WaterMarking (P2Mark) method for neural speech generation system protection. The main advantage of P2Mark is that the watermark information is flexibly integrated into the neural speech generation model in the form of parameters by training a watermark adapter rather than injecting the watermark into the model in the form of features. After the watermark adapter with the watermark embedding is merged with the pre-trained generation model, the watermark information cannot be easily removed or manipulated. Therefore, P2Mark will be a reliable choice for proactively tracing and protecting the copyrights of neural speech generation models in open-source white-box scenarios. We validated P2Mark on two main types of decoders in neural speech generation: vocoder and codec. Experimental results show that P2Mark achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art audio watermarking methods that cannot be used for open-source white-box protection scenarios in terms of watermark extraction accuracy, watermark imperceptibility, and robustness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes P2Mark, a "Plug-and-Play Parameter-intrinsic Watermarking" method for neural speech generation models, aiming to protect copyrights in open-source scenarios. P2Mark integrates watermark information into the model parameters via a trainable "watermark adapter" (WM-LoRA), rather than through feature injection.  This adapter is trained alongside a watermark encoder and decoder while keeping the original pre-trained model frozen.  After training, the adapter's weights are merged into the main model, making the watermark difficult to remove even with full code and model access.  The authors validate P2Mark on two common neural speech generation decoder types: vocoders (HiFi-GAN) and codec decoders (HiFi-Codec). They use a novel gradient orthogonal projection optimization (WGOPO) to reduce interference between watermark optimization and audio quality optimization. Results show comparable performance to existing watermarking methods in terms of accuracy, imperceptibility, and robustness, but with the added benefit of white-box security.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the parameter-intrinsic approach using a LoRA-style adapter for watermarking. Integrating the watermark into the *parameters* themselves offers a stronger defense against removal in open-source settings compared to feature-based methods that are easily bypassed by modifying the code. The plug-and-play aspect, allowing integration with existing models and flexible modification of the watermark *before* model release, is also a valuable contribution. The WGOPO optimization technique to minimize performance degradation due to watermarking, while not entirely novel conceptually (gradient projection is a known technique), is a useful addition and well-suited to the task.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and increasingly relevant problem: protecting intellectual property associated with open-source speech generation models.  As these models become more powerful and accessible, concerns about misuse and copyright infringement grow. P2Mark provides a tangible defense mechanism against attacks in scenarios where prior feature-based watermarking methods would be ineffective.  The method's validation across different decoder architectures (vocoder, codec) enhances its applicability and broadens its potential impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong problem motivation and clear explanation of the threat model.
    *   Technically sound approach leveraging parameter-efficient fine-tuning.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple metrics (accuracy, imperceptibility, robustness).
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the benefits of WGOPO.
    *   Well-written and clearly presented paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The robustness experiments, while including common attacks, lack a specific adversarial attack designed *to* remove the watermark. A more targeted removal attack would provide a more rigorous stress test of the method's security in white-box conditions.
    *   The experiments are limited to two base models. While representative, showing performance on additional, perhaps more complex, architectures would be beneficial.
    *   The capacity is currently capped at 32 bits, and a greater capacity would be useful in certain use cases.
    *   The paper doesn't explore how the location of the WM-LoRA module affects the robustness or audio quality.
    *   The comparison is limited, especially considering WMCodec (a state-of-the-art codec-based audio watermarking method, which already claims robustness and high-performance audio Post-hoc watermarking method based on Invertible Neural Networks).
    *   The paper mentions the use of gradient orthogonal projection, but it doesn't explain the importance of why they used AGEM, it would be much more useful to use one that is closer to the problem.

*   **Potential Influence:** P2Mark has the potential to influence the development and deployment of open-source speech generation models by providing a practical, white-box security solution. It could encourage broader sharing of models without the fear of copyright infringement. The concepts used here (LoRA-style adaptation, parameter-intrinsic watermarking, gradient projection) could be adapted to other generative model domains as well.

*   **Overall:** P2Mark is a solid contribution with demonstrable novelty and practical relevance. While there are some limitations in the experimental evaluation and scope, the approach is well-designed and addresses a significant gap in the protection of open-source speech generation models.

Score: 7

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's notable contributions while acknowledging its limitations. The concept of parameter-intrinsic watermarking with LoRA in this context is genuinely innovative and tackles a real-world problem effectively. However, the lack of adversarial removal attacks and limited exploration of model architectures prevent a higher score. The method’s strengths in white-box security, plug-and-play usability, and comparable performance to existing methods justify a score above average. Overall, the paper has a tangible impact on the field, but has scope for further strengthening.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quantum Program Linting with LLMs: Emerging Results from a Comparative Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05204v1)
- **Authors**: Seung Yeob Shin, Fabrizio Pastore, Domenico Bianculli
- **Abstract**: Ensuring the quality of quantum programs is increasingly important; however, traditional static analysis techniques are insufficient due to the unique characteristics of quantum computing. Quantum-specific linting tools, such as LintQ, have been developed to detect quantum-specific programming problems; however, they typically rely on manually crafted analysis queries. The manual effort required to update these tools limits their adaptability to evolving quantum programming practices. To address this challenge, this study investigates the feasibility of employing Large Language Models (LLMs) to develop a novel linting technique for quantum software development and explores potential avenues to advance linting approaches. We introduce LintQ-LLM, an LLM-based linting tool designed to detect quantum-specific problems comparable to those identified by LintQ. Through an empirical comparative study using real-world Qiskit programs, our results show that LintQ-LLM is a viable solution that complements LintQ, with particular strengths in problem localization, explanation clarity, and adaptability potential for emerging quantum programming frameworks, thus providing a basis for further research. Furthermore, this study discusses several research opportunities for developing more advanced, adaptable, and feedback-aware quantum software quality assurance methods by leveraging LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Quantum Program Linting with LLMs: Emerging Results from a Comparative Study" explores the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLMs) for linting quantum programs written in Qiskit. It introduces LintQ-LLM, an LLM-based linting tool, and compares its performance against LintQ, a state-of-the-art quantum-specific linting tool that relies on manually crafted queries. The study uses a dataset of real-world Qiskit programs to evaluate both tools. Results suggest that LintQ-LLM can detect quantum-specific problems, sometimes offering better problem localization and clearer explanations than LintQ.  While LintQ-LLM performs well on API-related issues, it struggles with more complex problems that require data flow analysis. The paper concludes by discussing the strengths and limitations of each approach and proposing future research directions, including hybrid linting approaches and LLM-assisted parser generation for new quantum programming languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in being the *first* exploration of using LLMs for quantum program linting.  While LLMs have been applied to classical software engineering tasks like code generation and bug detection, their application to the specialized domain of quantum computing, with its unique concepts and challenges, is a novel contribution. The introduction of LintQ-LLM as a concrete instantiation of this idea is a valuable contribution.

**Significance:**

The potential significance of the work stems from the fact that quantum programming is a nascent field where traditional software analysis techniques are often inadequate. Existing quantum-specific linting tools often rely on manually created rules or queries, which are difficult to maintain and adapt as the field evolves. LLMs offer a potential solution by leveraging their ability to learn from large codebases and generalize to new problems.

However, the current study has limitations that temper its immediate impact.  The experimental results show that LintQ-LLM isn't strictly superior to LintQ, especially in areas requiring complex data flow analysis. While the localization accuracy of the LLM is promising, the challenges in dealing with large files (due to token limits) is a significant constraint. The reliance on a relatively older LLM model (GPT-3.5 Turbo with a 2021 knowledge cutoff) to avoid potential bias is understandable but means the results might not reflect the capabilities of current state-of-the-art LLMs.

The paper also raises important questions for future research, such as how to best combine the strengths of rule-based and LLM-based linting, how to address the limitations of LLMs in complex analysis tasks, and how to create hybrid approaches.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Application:** Explores a new and relevant application of LLMs in the quantum computing domain.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** Provides a comparative study with a state-of-the-art tool, LintQ, using real-world Qiskit programs.
*   **Identifies Key Challenges:** Accurately points out the limitations of LLM-based linting, such as dealing with large files and complex data flow analysis.
*   **Raises Important Research Questions:** Proposes promising directions for future research, including hybrid approaches and LLM-assisted parser generation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM Capabilities in Complex Analysis:** The current LLM model struggles with tasks requiring complex data flow analysis.
*   **Token Limit Issues:** The token limit of GPT-3.5 Turbo restricts the size of the programs that can be analyzed.
*   **Reliance on an Older LLM:** The use of GPT-3.5 Turbo limits the applicability of the findings to modern LLM models.
*   **Threats to Validity:** The annotation dataset might be biased towards LintQ.
*   **Lack of Thorough Error Analysis:** A more detailed error analysis, categorized by different types of mistakes the LLM makes, would be helpful.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign this paper a score of **6**.

*   The novelty is a significant positive, as it's the first work I've seen applying LLMs to quantum linting.  This opens up a new avenue of research.
*   The comparative evaluation adds value, grounding the investigation in empirical results and providing a baseline for future work.
*   However, the demonstrated capabilities of LintQ-LLM, while promising, are not yet compelling enough to significantly displace or outperform existing techniques. The study reveals clear limitations (token limits, complex analysis), and there are concerns about the generalizability of the findings given the reliance on a specific LLM version and potential bias in the dataset.

The paper serves as a valuable proof-of-concept and a roadmap for future research, but more substantial improvements are needed to achieve a truly significant impact on the field. The identified weaknesses and limitations prevent a higher score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Post-Training Language Models for Continual Relation Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05214v1)
- **Authors**: Sefika Efeoglu, Adrian Paschke, Sonja Schimmler
- **Abstract**: Real-world data, such as news articles, social media posts, and chatbot conversations, is inherently dynamic and non-stationary, presenting significant challenges for constructing real-time structured representations through knowledge graphs (KGs). Relation Extraction (RE), a fundamental component of KG creation, often struggles to adapt to evolving data when traditional models rely on static, outdated datasets. Continual Relation Extraction (CRE) methods tackle this issue by incrementally learning new relations while preserving previously acquired knowledge. This study investigates the application of pre-trained language models (PLMs), specifically large language models (LLMs), to CRE, with a focus on leveraging memory replay to address catastrophic forgetting. We evaluate decoder-only models (eg, Mistral-7B and Llama2-7B) and encoder-decoder models (eg, Flan-T5 Base) on the TACRED and FewRel datasets. Task-incremental fine-tuning of LLMs demonstrates superior performance over earlier approaches using encoder-only models like BERT on TACRED, excelling in seen-task accuracy and overall performance (measured by whole and average accuracy), particularly with the Mistral and Flan-T5 models. Results on FewRel are similarly promising, achieving second place in whole and average accuracy metrics. This work underscores critical factors in knowledge transfer, language model architecture, and KG completeness, advancing CRE with LLMs and memory replay for dynamic, real-time relation extraction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the application of pre-trained language models (PLMs), specifically large language models (LLMs), to Continual Relation Extraction (CRE).  CRE deals with the problem of incrementally learning new relations while retaining knowledge of previously learned ones, addressing the challenges posed by real-world, non-stationary data. The authors focus on using memory replay to mitigate catastrophic forgetting in LLMs when fine-tuned incrementally. They evaluate decoder-only (Mistral-7B, Llama2-7B) and encoder-decoder (Flan-T5 Base) models on the TACRED and FewRel datasets. The study demonstrates that fine-tuning LLMs task-incrementally can outperform previous approaches, particularly with the Mistral and Flan-T5 models. The paper also explores the presence of backward knowledge transfer and addresses challenges encountered with the FewRel dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** CRE is a crucial area for real-world knowledge graph construction from dynamic data.  Applying LLMs to this task is a natural and timely progression.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a solid empirical evaluation of several prominent LLMs across two established datasets. The comparison with previous SOTA methods is valuable.
    *   **Backward Knowledge Transfer:** The analysis of backward knowledge transfer is an interesting and less commonly explored aspect in continual learning. The findings regarding Llama2 exhibiting positive backward transfer are noteworthy.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper delves into the specific challenges faced by different model architectures (e.g., Flan-T5's catastrophic forgetting on FewRel) and provides potential explanations based on dataset characteristics (e.g., token count). This level of analysis adds depth to the study.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper provides sufficient details regarding experiment setup, datasets and model parameters allowing for potential replication of the reported outcomes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** While applying LLMs to CRE is a relevant extension, the core technique (incremental fine-tuning with memory replay) is not inherently novel. Memory replay is a common strategy in continual learning, and the primary contribution lies in evaluating different LLMs within this framework.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The primary contribution lies in benchmarking performance with memory replay in a continual learning setup. Although the analyses regarding dataset characteristics of FewRel are insightful, the research does not provide significant advancement over existing methodologies.
    *   **Dataset Selection**: While the chosen datasets are popular benchmarks, they might not fully represent the complexities of real-world continual relation extraction tasks. Issues like evolving schemas and highly imbalanced relation distributions are not addressed in the experimental setup.
    *   **Memory Replay Simplification:** The K-means clustering for memory sample selection is a relatively simple approach. More sophisticated methods for selecting representative and diverse samples could potentially yield better results.
    *   **LoRA details**: While the LoRA settings are described, the decision to use a 4-bit quantized model is not discussed at length. It would be pertinent to include a discussion or ablation on the trade offs of utilising QLoRA during training.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a valuable benchmark for using LLMs in CRE tasks. The results offer practical insights into the performance of different model architectures and highlight the importance of addressing catastrophic forgetting.
    *   The identification of backward knowledge transfer in Llama2 is an intriguing finding that could inspire further research into mechanisms for improving knowledge retention and consolidation in LLMs.
    *   The paper motivates further exploration of more advanced memory replay techniques and the development of CRE methods specifically tailored to LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a useful but not groundbreaking contribution. The evaluation of LLMs for CRE is relevant, and the analysis of backward knowledge transfer adds some novelty. However, the core methodology relies on existing continual learning techniques (memory replay), and the practical impact might be limited without addressing the challenges inherent in real-world CRE scenarios.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unleashing the Power of LLMs in Dense Retrieval with Query Likelihood Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05216v1)
- **Authors**: Hengran Zhang, Keping Bi, Jiafeng Guo, Xiaojie Sun, Shihao Liu, Daiting Shi, Dawei Yin, Xueqi Cheng
- **Abstract**: Dense retrieval is a crucial task in Information Retrieval (IR) and is the foundation for downstream tasks such as re-ranking. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have shown compelling semantic understanding capabilities and are appealing to researchers studying dense retrieval. LLMs, as decoder-style generative models, are competent at language generation while falling short on modeling global information due to the lack of attention to tokens afterward. Inspired by the classical word-based language modeling approach for IR, i.e., the query likelihood (QL) model, we seek to sufficiently utilize LLMs' generative ability by QL maximization. However, instead of ranking documents with QL estimation, we introduce an auxiliary task of QL maximization to yield a better backbone for contrastively learning a discriminative retriever. We name our model as LLM-QL. To condense global document semantics to a single vector during QL modeling, LLM-QL has two major components, Attention Stop (AS) and Input Corruption (IC). AS stops the attention of predictive tokens to previous tokens until the ending token of the document. IC masks a portion of tokens in the input documents during prediction. Experiments on MSMARCO show that LLM-QL can achieve significantly better performance than other LLM-based retrievers and using QL estimated by LLM-QL for ranking outperforms word-based QL by a large margin.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Unleashing the Power of LLMs in Dense Retrieval with Query Likelihood Modeling" introduces LLM-QL, a novel approach to dense information retrieval that leverages the generative capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating query likelihood (QL) modeling. LLM-QL combines query likelihood learning with contrastive fine-tuning.  The core idea is to use LLMs to generate potential queries given document content and then use this generated query information to improve dense document representations.  Two key components enhance LLM-QL: Attention Stop (AS), which limits attention during query generation, and Input Corruption (IC), which masks input document tokens. The method is evaluated on MSMARCO and other datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to other LLM-based retrievers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the *combination* of LLMs' generative capabilities with the classic query likelihood model within a dense retrieval framework. Using LLMs as generators to inform dense retrievers is not entirely new, but the specific implementation with Attention Stop and Input Corruption is a differentiating factor. The idea of adapting the query likelihood framework to LLMs is a conceptually interesting move.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing a known limitation of LLMs in information retrieval: their tendency to focus on local context and lack of global semantic representation. By using the query likelihood objective as an auxiliary task, the model is pushed to better condense document semantics and capture potential query needs, which can improve downstream search performance. The improved performance on established benchmark datasets suggests real-world applicability. However, the significance is somewhat tempered by the reliance on existing techniques like contrastive learning and LoRA, making LLM-QL more of an incremental improvement than a radical departure.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear motivation:** The paper articulates a clear problem (limitations of LLMs in dense retrieval) and proposes a reasonable solution (LLM-QL).
    *   **Well-defined method:** LLM-QL, with its Attention Stop and Input Corruption components, is well-defined and clearly explained.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on MSMARCO and other datasets, demonstrating improved performance over baselines.
    *   **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of each component of LLM-QL.
    *   **Case studies:**  The case studies provide a qualitative assessment of the generated queries from LLM-QL.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental novelty:**  The core components are built on existing techniques. The novelty is primarily in the integration and adaptation for the specific problem.
    *   **Limited investigation of pretraining data:** The paper acknowledges the potential of using LLM-QL as a large-scale pretraining method but does not explore this aspect in detail. This limits the evaluation of how LLM-QL can be utilized for creating effective pretraining tasks for LLMs.
    *   **Complex experimental setup:** The experimental setup with multiple stages (QL learning, contrastive fine-tuning) and various training techniques (LoRA, different negative sampling strategies) makes it somewhat difficult to isolate the exact contribution of each component.
    *   **Efficiency limitations:** The results on inference time should be included.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in dense retrieval by highlighting the value of incorporating generative models and classic IR techniques into LLM-based approaches. The code availability increases the reproducibility and impact of the work. The investigation of Attention Stop and Input Corruption may also lead to novel architectures and training strategies for LLMs in other tasks.

*   **Justification of Score:** LLM-QL offers a practical and effective way to improve the performance of LLMs in dense retrieval. However, the method is built upon existing concepts and architectures. The paper's value is in its careful combination of existing techniques and empirical validation on standard benchmarks. It's a solid engineering effort that improves upon the state-of-the-art rather than revolutionizing it.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging LLMs for Utility-Focused Annotation: Reducing Manual Effort for Retrieval and RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05220v2)
- **Authors**: Hengran Zhang, Minghao Tang, Keping Bi, Jiafeng Guo, Shihao Liu, Daiting Shi, Dawei Yin, Xueqi Cheng
- **Abstract**: Retrieval models typically rely on costly human-labeled query-document relevance annotations for training and evaluation. To reduce this cost and leverage the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) in relevance judgments, we aim to explore whether LLM-generated annotations can effectively replace human annotations in training retrieval models. Retrieval usually emphasizes relevance, which indicates "topic-relatedness" of a document to a query, while in RAG, the value of a document (or utility) depends on how it contributes to answer generation. Recognizing this mismatch, some researchers use LLM performance on downstream tasks with documents as labels, but this approach requires manual answers for specific tasks, leading to high costs and limited generalization. In another line of work, prompting LLMs to select useful documents as RAG references eliminates the need for human annotation and is not task-specific. If we leverage LLMs' utility judgments to annotate retrieval data, we may retain cross-task generalization without human annotation in large-scale corpora. Therefore, we investigate utility-focused annotation via LLMs for large-scale retriever training data across both in-domain and out-of-domain settings on the retrieval and RAG tasks. To reduce the impact of low-quality positives labeled by LLMs, we design a novel loss function, i.e., Disj-InfoNCE. Our experiments reveal that: (1) Retrievers trained on utility-focused annotations significantly outperform those trained on human annotations in the out-of-domain setting on both tasks, demonstrating superior generalization capabilities. (2) LLM annotation does not replace human annotation in the in-domain setting. However, incorporating just 20% human-annotated data enables retrievers trained with utility-focused annotations to match the performance of models trained entirely with human annotations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate annotations for training retrieval models, aiming to reduce the reliance on expensive human-labeled data. The key idea is to leverage LLMs to assess the "utility" of documents in a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) context rather than traditional relevance. This focuses on how well a document contributes to answer generation, potentially offering better generalization across tasks compared to relevance-based annotation or task-specific labels. The authors introduce a utility-focused annotation pipeline, a novel loss function (Disj-InfoNCE) to mitigate the impact of low-quality LLM-generated positives, and conduct experiments on retrieval and RAG tasks in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings. They find that models trained on utility-focused annotations can outperform human-annotated models in out-of-domain settings and can match performance in-domain with a small amount of human data used in a curriculum learning approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. The use of LLMs for annotation is not entirely new. However, the focus on "utility" instead of traditional "relevance" for training retrieval models in a RAG context is a valuable shift. The Disj-InfoNCE loss function designed to handle potentially noisy LLM-generated labels is also a novel contribution.  The curriculum learning approach, while not entirely novel, is effectively applied in this context to combine LLM and human annotations.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to reduce the cost and effort associated with training retrieval models, particularly for RAG applications. The ability to achieve strong out-of-domain performance using LLM-generated utility annotations is a significant advantage. The study demonstrates a practical way to leverage LLMs for large-scale data annotation when human annotations are limited or unavailable. The insights regarding the impact of loss functions and curriculum learning further contribute to the field.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem statement and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the mismatch between traditional relevance and the needs of RAG systems, justifying the focus on utility.
    * **Comprehensive experiments:** The authors conduct a thorough set of experiments across various datasets and settings, including both retrieval and RAG tasks, in-domain and out-of-domain evaluations.
    * **Practical solutions:** The proposed utility-focused annotation pipeline and Disj-InfoNCE loss function offer practical solutions to the challenges of using LLMs for annotation.
    * **Demonstrated out-of-domain generalization:** The paper shows compelling evidence that utility-focused annotations lead to better out-of-domain performance compared to human annotations.
    * **Valuable insights:** The paper provides valuable insights into the effectiveness of different annotation strategies, loss functions, and curriculum learning techniques when using LLMs for data annotation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **LLM annotation bias:** The paper acknowledges the potential for bias in LLM-generated annotations but doesn't fully address this issue.  Further analysis and techniques to mitigate bias would strengthen the paper.  The evaluation on the LLM test collection attempts to mitigate this but further exploration is needed.
    * **Limited analysis of the "utility" judgments:** While the paper introduces the concept of utility, a deeper analysis of what factors influence LLMs' utility judgments and how these factors align with downstream task performance would be beneficial. What features do LLMs use to assess Utility?
    * **Limited details on LLM Prompting:** The specifics of LLM prompting could be expanded further, given the importance of the annotation pipeline on the effectiveness of the retrieval model.
    * **Dependence on LLM performance:** The method's effectiveness heavily relies on the quality of the LLM used for annotation. Improvements to LLMs will inherently improve this methodology.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by:
    * Shifting focus towards utility-focused annotation for RAG systems.
    * Encouraging the development of methods for handling noisy LLM-generated labels.
    * Promoting the use of curriculum learning to combine LLM and human annotations.
    * Motivating further exploration of bias mitigation techniques in LLM-based data annotation.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the paper's moderate novelty, significant practical value, clear methodology, comprehensive experiments, and potential for impact in the field, while acknowledging its limitations regarding LLM bias and further analysis, a score of 7 is appropriate. It builds upon existing work in LLM-based annotation but introduces a valuable and practical twist focusing on utility for RAG.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-based Automated Grading with Human-in-the-Loop
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05239v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Li, Yucheng Chu, Kaiqi Yang, Yasemin Copur-Gencturk, Jiliang Tang
- **Abstract**: The rise of artificial intelligence (AI) technologies, particularly large language models (LLMs), has brought significant advancements to the field of education. Among various applications, automatic short answer grading (ASAG), which focuses on evaluating open-ended textual responses, has seen remarkable progress with the introduction of LLMs. These models not only enhance grading performance compared to traditional ASAG approaches but also move beyond simple comparisons with predefined "golden" answers, enabling more sophisticated grading scenarios, such as rubric-based evaluation. However, existing LLM-powered methods still face challenges in achieving human-level grading performance in rubric-based assessments due to their reliance on fully automated approaches. In this work, we explore the potential of LLMs in ASAG tasks by leveraging their interactive capabilities through a human-in-the-loop (HITL) approach. Our proposed framework, GradeHITL, utilizes the generative properties of LLMs to pose questions to human experts, incorporating their insights to refine grading rubrics dynamically. This adaptive process significantly improves grading accuracy, outperforming existing methods and bringing ASAG closer to human-level evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GradeHITL, a novel framework for automatic short answer grading (ASAG) that incorporates a human-in-the-loop (HITL) approach.  Instead of relying solely on automated large language models (LLMs), GradeHITL uses LLMs to generate questions about grading rubrics, solicits answers from human experts, and refines the rubrics dynamically based on these insights. This iterative process aims to improve grading accuracy and overcome the limitations of fully automated ASAG systems, particularly in rubric-based assessments where subtle domain knowledge and nuanced understanding are crucial. The framework leverages reinforcement learning to select the most valuable questions for human input and a multi-agent system to refine the rubrics. Experimental results on a pedagogical dataset demonstrate that GradeHITL outperforms existing automated methods and brings ASAG closer to human-level evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Significant Problem:** The paper tackles a real and important challenge in education: achieving human-level performance in automated essay grading, especially in complex subjects where rubric interpretation is key.  Fully automated systems often struggle with nuanced understanding and context.
*   **Novel Approach:** The HITL approach is a significant step forward. It moves beyond simply using LLMs to generate grades and instead leverages their interactive capabilities to clarify and refine the grading process.  This addresses a key limitation of relying solely on pre-labeled data, which can be insufficient for capturing the subtleties of expert knowledge.
*   **Well-Designed Framework:** The GradeHITL framework appears well-structured, with clearly defined components (Grading, Inquiring, Optimizing) and a logical workflow.  The use of reinforcement learning for question selection is a clever way to minimize human workload and prioritize impactful feedback. The multi-agent rubric refinement is based on the GradeOpt method, a state of the art approach that helps to improve the whole procedure.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents empirical evidence demonstrating the effectiveness of GradeHITL. The use of a pedagogical dataset focused on nuanced short-answer responses adds credibility. The comparison to relevant baselines, including both non-LLM and fully automated LLM-based methods, strengthens the claims.
*   **Reproducibility Considerations:** The authors included links to resources for reproduction of their results, which enhances the value and impact of the paper.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Specificity:** The evaluation is performed on a relatively specific type of pedagogical dataset.  While this allows for in-depth analysis of rubric-based grading, it raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other domains (e.g., scientific writing, creative writing, etc.).  The reliance on teachers' pedagogical knowledge might limit its applicability elsewhere.
*   **Limited Human Evaluation:** The core of the HITL approach relies on the quality and consistency of human input. The details on how the "grading expert" was chosen and trained are not sufficiently detailed. In an extreme case, results of the method may be affected by the reliability of the grading expert. A more thorough discussion of the human element in the loop is warranted.
*   **Complexity and Scalability:** The framework is relatively complex, involving multiple LLM agents, reinforcement learning, and human interaction. This complexity could make it challenging to implement and scale in real-world educational settings. Practical concerns like the time commitment required from experts and the cost of running multiple LLM instances are not thoroughly addressed.
*   **GPT-4 Dependency:** The experiments rely on GPT-4, a powerful but closed-source LLM. This creates a barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners who may not have access to this model. The generalizability to other, more accessible LLMs is unclear.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its HITL approach to ASAG, combining the strengths of LLMs with the insights of human experts.  While individual components (LLM-based grading, reinforcement learning) are not entirely new, their integration within the GradeHITL framework constitutes a significant contribution. The paper's significance is in demonstrating a pathway towards more accurate, reliable, and controllable ASAG systems, particularly in contexts where nuanced rubric interpretation is essential. The system also aims to overcome the problem of having jargons or domain-specific terms in rubric texts by soliciting feedback from human experts.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper tackles a relevant problem with a novel approach, pushing beyond existing automated methods.
*   The experimental results support the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
*   However, the limitations related to dataset specificity, human evaluation, complexity, and reliance on a specific LLM (GPT-4) hold the paper back from being a truly exceptional contribution. Addressing these weaknesses would significantly increase the paper's impact and generalizability.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Explaining Low Perception Model Competency with High-Competency Counterfactuals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05254v1)
- **Authors**: Sara Pohland, Claire Tomlin
- **Abstract**: There exist many methods to explain how an image classification model generates its decision, but very little work has explored methods to explain why a classifier might lack confidence in its prediction. As there are various reasons the classifier might lose confidence, it would be valuable for this model to not only indicate its level of uncertainty but also explain why it is uncertain. Counterfactual images have been used to visualize changes that could be made to an image to generate a different classification decision. In this work, we explore the use of counterfactuals to offer an explanation for low model competency--a generalized form of predictive uncertainty that measures confidence. Toward this end, we develop five novel methods to generate high-competency counterfactual images, namely Image Gradient Descent (IGD), Feature Gradient Descent (FGD), Autoencoder Reconstruction (Reco), Latent Gradient Descent (LGD), and Latent Nearest Neighbors (LNN). We evaluate these methods across two unique datasets containing images with six known causes for low model competency and find Reco, LGD, and LNN to be the most promising methods for counterfactual generation. We further evaluate how these three methods can be utilized by pre-trained Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to generate language explanations for low model competency. We find that the inclusion of a counterfactual image in the language model query greatly increases the ability of the model to generate an accurate explanation for the cause of low model competency, thus demonstrating the utility of counterfactual images in explaining low perception model competency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of explaining why an image classification model lacks confidence in its predictions, a less explored area compared to explaining confident predictions.  The authors propose using counterfactual images, which are similar to the original low-confidence image but have high model competency, as a tool for explanation.  They introduce five novel methods for generating these high-competency counterfactuals: Image Gradient Descent (IGD), Feature Gradient Descent (FGD), Autoencoder Reconstruction (Reco), Latent Gradient Descent (LGD), and Latent Nearest Neighbors (LNN). They evaluate these methods across two datasets and then use pre-trained Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), primarily LLaMA, to generate language explanations based on these counterfactuals.  The key finding is that including a counterfactual image significantly improves the accuracy of the MLLM in explaining the cause of low model competency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution lies in its framing of the problem – explaining *low* model competency. While counterfactuals are a well-established tool in explainable AI, their application to this specific problem is novel.  The proposed counterfactual generation methods, especially the latent space-based ones (LGD and LNN), offer potentially useful ways to create images that highlight the factors affecting confidence. Combining counterfactual images with MLLMs for explanation is also a novel approach for this particular problem.

*   **Significance:**  Understanding model uncertainty is crucial for deploying AI systems in real-world scenarios.  Being able to not only *detect* uncertainty but also *explain* it makes models more trustworthy and easier to debug. The paper's approach could lead to more informative feedback mechanisms for models, enabling active learning and improved robustness. The paper also contributes practically to the field by providing novel methods for generating counterfactual images to offer explanations for model competency.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Framing:** The paper clearly defines and motivates the problem of explaining low model competency.
    *   **Methodology:** It presents a well-defined methodology, with clear descriptions of the counterfactual generation methods and the integration with MLLMs.
    *   **Experimental Setup:**  The use of two distinct datasets and a range of metrics enhances the validity of the findings.
    *   **Results:** The results convincingly demonstrate the utility of counterfactuals in improving the accuracy of MLLM-generated explanations.
    *   **Generality:** Despite focusing on images, the general approach of generating counterfactuals for understanding uncertainty could be applicable to other data types and models.
    *   **Open Code:** The availability of code will allow the field to build upon the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **MLLM Reliance:** The explanations heavily rely on the capabilities of the pre-trained LLaMA model. The study is limited by the model’s inherent biases and limitations.
    *   **Manual Evaluation:** Manually evaluating the "correctness" of language explanations is subjective and prone to bias. More rigorous, quantitative evaluation metrics or user studies would strengthen the findings. The paper notes some explanations still hallucinates.
    *   **Limited Datasets:** Although they use two datasets, the choice of lunar and speed limit datasets, while distinct, can be argued as not reflecting the diversity of real world datasets for competency.
    *   **Counterfactual Image Quality:**  While LGD and LNN perform well, the visual quality of counterfactuals generated can be still improved, especially for noisy and pixelated images. As the study notes, sometimes, counterfactual images contain unrealistic artifacts or don't address the true cause of low competency.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could encourage further research into explainable AI techniques focused on model uncertainty and confidence.  It demonstrates the potential of combining counterfactuals with language models for generating interpretable explanations. It may also spur the development of more robust methods for generating high-quality counterfactual images, specifically designed for explanation purposes.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**. The paper is strong and makes a novel contribution to a less-explored area in explainable AI, applying counterfactuals to low model competency and linking with large language models. Its methodology is sound, and the results are compelling. However, the reliance on LLaMA and a manual evaluation process, and potentially limited dataset diversity, slightly temper the strength of the findings. Future work to address these weaknesses could significantly increase the paper's impact.

### Learning to Reason Over Time: Timeline Self-Reflection for Improved Temporal Reasoning in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05258v1)
- **Authors**: Adrián Bazaga, Rexhina Blloshmi, Bill Byrne, Adrià de Gispert
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for generating coherent text, understanding context, and performing reasoning tasks. However, they struggle with temporal reasoning, which requires processing time-related information such as event sequencing, durations, and inter-temporal relationships. These capabilities are critical for applications including question answering, scheduling, and historical analysis. In this paper, we introduce TISER, a novel framework that enhances the temporal reasoning abilities of LLMs through a multi-stage process that combines timeline construction with iterative self-reflection. Our approach leverages test-time scaling to extend the length of reasoning traces, enabling models to capture complex temporal dependencies more effectively. This strategy not only boosts reasoning accuracy but also improves the traceability of the inference process. Experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across multiple benchmarks, including out-of-distribution test sets, and reveal that TISER enables smaller open-source models to surpass larger closed-weight models on challenging temporal reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TISER, a novel framework designed to improve temporal reasoning abilities in Large Language Models (LLMs).  TISER employs a multi-stage inference pipeline that combines explicit reasoning, timeline construction, and iterative self-reflection.  It leverages test-time scaling to extend reasoning traces, enabling LLMs to capture complex temporal dependencies more effectively.  The authors augment existing temporal reasoning benchmarks with detailed intermediate reasoning traces for fine-tuning. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks, including out-of-distribution datasets, and showcase that smaller open-source models fine-tuned with TISER can outperform larger closed-weight models on challenging temporal reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of combining timeline construction and iterative self-reflection is novel in the context of temporal reasoning for LLMs.  While CoT and self-reflection are individually known techniques, their integration with explicit timeline building and test-time scaling for *this specific problem* provides a unique approach. The introduction of a synthetic dataset with intermediate reasoning traces is also a valuable contribution. A strength here is the pragmatic focus; the authors didn't just propose a theoretical method, they built a concrete framework with code and data that other researchers can readily use.

* **Significance:** Temporal reasoning is a known weakness of LLMs, and improving it has broad implications for applications like question answering, scheduling, and historical analysis. Demonstrating that smaller open-source models can outperform larger, closed-source models using TISER is a significant finding. This suggests a potential path towards more efficient and accessible temporal reasoning capabilities.  The paper also improves the traceability of the inference process. Making reasoning steps explicit allows for debugging and understanding model behavior, increasing trust and reliability. However, the reliance on a synthetic dataset could be a weakness. It's possible that the gains observed are partially due to overfitting to the synthetic data structure, which might not fully generalize to real-world unstructured text.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Framework:** The TISER framework is clearly defined and well-explained.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks, including OOD.
    *   **Practical Contribution:** The creation of a synthetic dataset with reasoning traces is a valuable resource for the community.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies help to understand the contribution of each component.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Data Reliance:** The reliance on a *synthetically generated* dataset with intermediate traces for fine-tuning raises questions about generalization to real-world scenarios. There is a concern of the model simply memorizing the patterns and structures within the augmented training data.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** The paper acknowledges increased computational overhead due to the multi-stage pipeline, which may limit its applicability in resource-constrained environments.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus is on text-only temporal reasoning, leaving out other forms of abstract or multi-modal reasoning.
    *   **Lack of Thorough Error Analysis:** A deeper analysis of the types of temporal errors TISER successfully mitigates, and the types of errors it still struggles with, would strengthen the paper.

* **Potential Influence:** If TISER proves to generalize well beyond the synthetic training data, it could become a standard technique for enhancing temporal reasoning in LLMs.  The framework's emphasis on interpretability and traceability could also influence the development of more trustworthy AI systems. The paper can lead to a new line of work on data augmentation techniques for LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7/10. The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem, and the empirical results are compelling. The finding that smaller open-source models can outperform larger closed-source models is particularly noteworthy. However, the reliance on synthetic training data and the increased computational overhead are significant limitations that prevent a higher score.  Furthermore, a more thorough analysis of failure cases and generalization would strengthen the claims. While the work presents solid advancements in the temporal reasoning abilities of LLMs, there is room for further exploration and validation.

- **Classification**: cs.LG

### Do PhD-level LLMs Truly Grasp Elementary Addition? Probing Rule Learning vs. Memorization in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05262v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Yan, Yu Lu, Renjun Xu, Zhenzhong Lan
- **Abstract**: Despite high benchmark scores, Large Language Models (LLMs) often fail simple problem, raising a critical question: Do LLMs learn mathematical principles or merely memorize patterns? Rather than designing increasingly complex benchmarks like recent works, we investigate this using elementary two-integer addition ($0$ to $2^{64}$), probing two core properties: commutativity ($A+B=B+A$) and compositional generalization (via isomorphic symbolic mappings, e.g., $7 \rightarrow y$). While state-of-the-art LLMs achieve 73.8-99.8\% accuracy on numerical addition, performance collapses to $\leq$7.5\% under symbolic mapping, indicating failure to generalize learned rules. Non-monotonic performance scaling with digit count and frequent commutativity violations (over 1,700 cases of $A+B \neq B+A$) further support this. Explicitly providing addition rules degrades performance by 81.2\% on average, while self-explanation maintains baseline accuracy, suggesting LLM arithmetic processing is misaligned with human-defined principles. Our findings indicate current LLMs rely on memory pattern over genuine rule learning, highlighting architectural limitations and the need for new approaches to achieve true mathematical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Do PhD-level LLMs Truly Grasp Elementary Addition? Probing Rule Learning vs. Memorization in Large Language Models" investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) actually understand the fundamental principles of addition or if they simply memorize patterns from training data.  Instead of using complex mathematical benchmarks, the authors focus on elementary two-integer addition.  They probe two core properties: commutativity (A + B = B + A) and compositional generalization (using isomorphic symbolic mappings, e.g., 7 -> Y). The study reveals that while LLMs perform well on numerical addition, their performance significantly degrades when using symbolic mappings, and they frequently violate the commutativity property. Providing explicit addition rules also paradoxically degrades performance, suggesting a conflict between externally provided rules and internalized knowledge. The authors conclude that LLMs primarily rely on memorization and pattern matching rather than genuine rule-based reasoning for addition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focused and controlled approach to evaluating LLMs' understanding of a seemingly trivial task – elementary addition. Most prior work concentrates on complex mathematical reasoning, which can obscure fundamental issues. The use of symbolic mapping to disrupt pattern matching and reveal underlying weaknesses is a strong and original contribution. Probing commutativity is also a simple yet effective method to expose underlying issues in how LLMs perform addition.
* **Significance:**  The paper has significant implications for how we interpret LLM performance on mathematical tasks. It suggests that high scores on benchmarks might be misleading, reflecting pattern recognition rather than true understanding.  This challenges the assumption that scaling up model size alone will lead to genuine reasoning abilities.  The paper highlights the need for new evaluation methodologies that can distinguish between memorization and true algorithmic comprehension. Showing that explicitly providing rules degrades performance is a crucial finding that indicates a misalignment between model computation and mathematical cognition and therefore warrants more investigation.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and Focused Research Question:** The paper clearly defines its research question and approach.
    * **Controlled Experiments:** The experimental design is well-controlled, isolating the effects of different factors (digit length, symbolic mapping, rule provision).
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study evaluates a range of state-of-the-art LLMs, strengthening the generality of its findings.
    * **Thoughtful Analysis:** The paper provides a thoughtful discussion of the implications of the results and suggests directions for future research.
    * **Reproducible Methodology:** The methodology appears well-defined and reproducible.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** While focusing on addition is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings to other mathematical operations. One could argue that addition, due to its repetitive nature, is particularly susceptible to pattern matching.
    * **Zero-Shot Setting Limitation:** While the zero-shot setting is useful for establishing baselines, it might not fully reflect the potential of LLMs when fine-tuned or prompted with relevant examples. It would strengthen the paper to explore few-shot performance with more emphasis on symbolic addition.
    * **Explanation of Negative Results:** While providing explicit rules reduces performance, the exact mechanism driving this phenomenon is not fully explained. Is it interference with learned patterns, a failure to properly parse the rules, or something else? More analysis in this area would be valuable.
    * **Over claiming true "understanding"**: The claim that LLMs can achieve a form of true "understanding" is possibly too high a bar. It might have been more effective to frame the results in light of what strategies are used in performing these tasks.
    * **Dataset generation for specific probes.** The probe design for the experiments seems hand crafted but could have included automated techniques.

* **Impact and Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on evaluating LLMs' mathematical capabilities. It could inspire the development of new benchmarks and evaluation methods that are more sensitive to rule-based reasoning. It also encourages researchers to consider alternative architectural designs that promote genuine understanding over pattern recognition. The work is already generating discussion within the LLM research community.

**Overall:**

This is a solid paper with a clear message, well-designed experiments, and significant implications for the field. While there are limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. It addresses a fundamental concern about LLM reasoning and provides valuable insights for future research.
