*   **Significance:**  Mesh generation is a crucial task for many applications, and addressing issues like unrealistic patterns, missing parts, and incomplete structures has practical relevance. The paper shows promising results in terms of mesh quality and watertightness, which are critical factors for downstream tasks like rendering and simulation. The performance of the approach is reasonable, but the gains compared to GenUDC, which the authors position as the prior state-of-the-art, are not consistently significant across all metrics. Additionally, while the authors claim to generate meshes with both detailed fidelity and watertight surfaces, qualitative visual inspection reveals that the overall appearance can lack detail in comparison to real shapes.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Effective use of PoNQ representation for detailed local geometry.
    *   Multi-decoder VAE designed to produce the PoNQ features.
    *   Demonstrated capability to generate watertight meshes, which is a significant advantage.
    *   Quantitative results demonstrating competitiveness with state-of-the-art methods, especially for MMD.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental rather than revolutionary advancement.
    *   The advantage over existing state-of-the-art (GenUDC) is not consistently dominant across all metrics.
    *   Qualitative results, while good, can lack fine detail compared to ground-truth shapes or other methods.
    *   The qualitative evaluation is not rigorous enough, as they don't provide comprehensive comparisons across all objects.

*   **Impact:** The paper presents a novel approach to mesh generation that could influence future research in this area. However, given the incremental nature of the advancement and the limited performance, its overall impact may be moderate. Further research is needed to improve the generated detail and demonstrate clear and substantial gains over existing techniques.

* **Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by combining PoNQ and diffusion models for mesh generation. The approach has a reasonable novelty in the architectural setup, and produces decent results, as evidenced by the results from both qualitative and quantitative experiments. However, the paper's impact is limited due to the incremental nature of the method. Because the improvement, although present, is not substantial. Also, there are limitations concerning detail rendering and the significance of superiority to SOTA.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unified Generative Search and Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05730v1)
- **Authors**: Teng Shi, Jun Xu, Xiao Zhang, Xiaoxue Zang, Kai Zheng, Yang Song, Enyun Yu
- **Abstract**: Modern commercial platforms typically offer both search and recommendation functionalities to serve diverse user needs, making joint modeling of these tasks an appealing direction. While prior work has shown that integrating search and recommendation can be mutually beneficial, it also reveals a performance trade-off: enhancements in one task often come at the expense of the other. This challenge arises from their distinct information requirements: search emphasizes semantic relevance between queries and items, whereas recommendation depends more on collaborative signals among users and items. Effectively addressing this trade-off requires tackling two key problems: (1) integrating both semantic and collaborative signals into item representations, and (2) guiding the model to distinguish and adapt to the unique demands of search and recommendation. The emergence of generative retrieval with Large Language Models (LLMs) presents new possibilities. This paradigm encodes items as identifiers and frames both search and recommendation as sequential generation tasks, offering the flexibility to leverage multiple identifiers and task-specific prompts. In light of this, we introduce GenSAR, a unified generative framework for balanced search and recommendation. Our approach designs dual-purpose identifiers and tailored training strategies to incorporate complementary signals and align with task-specific objectives. Experiments on both public and commercial datasets demonstrate that GenSAR effectively reduces the trade-off and achieves state-of-the-art performance on both tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GenSAR, a unified generative framework for jointly modeling search and recommendation (S&R) tasks. It addresses the performance trade-off often observed in joint S&R models, where improvements in one task come at the expense of the other. GenSAR leverages large language models (LLMs) and generative retrieval to represent items as identifiers, formulating both search and recommendation as sequence generation tasks. The key innovations are: (1) designing dual-purpose identifiers that integrate both semantic (for search) and collaborative (for recommendation) signals, and (2) using task-specific training strategies to guide the LLM to distinguish and adapt to the unique demands of each task. Experiments on public and commercial datasets demonstrate that GenSAR effectively reduces the S&R trade-off and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper offers a reasonably novel approach to the joint S&R problem by framing it within the generative retrieval paradigm using LLMs.  The dual identifier concept—combining shared, semantic, and collaborative codes—is a good idea. However, the application of RQ-VAE and sequence-to-sequence generation isn't entirely groundbreaking in itself, and many elements can be found in prior work on search or recommendation with LLMs. The main novelty lies in the specific combination and adaptation of these techniques to address the S&R trade-off.

*   **Significance:** The problem of balancing search and recommendation performance is significant for commercial platforms. If the performance figures are replicable in deployment (particularly the commercial dataset), the practical implications could be substantial. The ablation studies provide good insight into the contribution of the different components, although the commercial dataset is proprietary and makes independent verification impossible.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture and training strategies.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on both public and commercial datasets.
    *   Ablation studies that provide insight into the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core techniques (RQ-VAE, sequence-to-sequence generation) aren't entirely novel.
    *   The commercial dataset is proprietary, hindering independent verification.
    *   The paper's claim to 'eliminate' trade-off is hyperbolic, although it reduces it.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper addresses a practical problem in a well-structured and empirical manner, demonstrating significant performance improvements compared to existing models. The dual identifier and task-specific objectives contribute novel perspectives in this joint space. Although the technical novelty may not be revolutionary, the integration and the empirical results provide a useful contribution to the joint S&R space.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Retrieval Augmented Generation with Collaborative Filtering for Personalized Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05731v1)
- **Authors**: Teng Shi, Jun Xu, Xiao Zhang, Xiaoxue Zang, Kai Zheng, Yang Song, Han Li
- **Abstract**: Recently, the personalization of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate content that aligns with individual user preferences has garnered widespread attention. Personalized Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), which retrieves relevant documents from the user's history to reflect their preferences and enhance LLM generation, is one commonly used approach for personalization. However, existing personalized RAG methods do not consider that the histories of similar users can also assist in personalized generation for the current user, meaning that collaborative information between users can also benefit personalized generation. Inspired by the application of collaborative filtering in recommender systems, we propose a method called CFRAG, which adapts Collaborative Filtering to RAG for personalized text generation. However, this presents two challenges: (1)~how to incorporate collaborative information without explicit user similarity labels? (2)~how to retrieve documents that support personalized LLM generation? For Challenge 1, we use contrastive learning to train user embeddings to retrieve similar users and introduce collaborative information. For Challenge 2, we design a personalized retriever and reranker to retrieve the top-$k$ documents from these users' histories. We take into account the user's preference during retrieval and reranking. Then we leverage feedback from the LLM to fine-tune the personalized retriever and reranker, enabling them to retrieve documents that meet the personalized generation needs of the LLM. Experimental results on the Language Model Personalization (LaMP) benchmark validate the effectiveness of CFRAG. Further analysis confirms the importance of incorporating collaborative information.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes CFRAG, a novel method for personalized text generation using Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with collaborative filtering.  It addresses the limitation of existing personalized RAG methods that only leverage a single user's history.  CFRAG incorporates information from similar users to improve personalization. The key contributions are:

1.  **User Retrieval:**  A contrastive learning approach to train user embeddings and retrieve similar users, tackling the challenge of incorporating collaborative information without explicit user similarity labels.
2.  **Personalized Retriever & Reranker:**  Designing a retriever and reranker that considers both semantic relevance and user preferences, which are fine-tuned using LLM feedback.

The paper validates CFRAG's effectiveness on the Language Model Personalization (LaMP) benchmark, demonstrating its superior performance compared to existing methods and highlighting the importance of collaborative information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of collaborative filtering with RAG for personalized text generation.  Existing personalized RAG focuses primarily on the individual user's history, whereas CFRAG takes a step towards leveraging group knowledge, adapting techniques commonly used in recommender systems. The use of contrastive learning to derive user similarity without explicit labels is a sensible approach to solve the core challenge. Using LLM feedback to improve the retriever and reranker is a solid step to align generated text with user preferences.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it addresses a clear limitation in existing personalized RAG approaches. By considering collaborative information, the method has the potential to generate more relevant and accurate outputs for a given user. The experimental results on the LaMP benchmark provide empirical support for the effectiveness of the proposed method. However, the improvement gains over other RAG approaches seem moderate.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined approach with a combination of techniques.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation using a relevant benchmark.
    *   Ablation studies to validate the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited exploration of alternative methods for collaborative filtering. While contrastive learning is reasonable, there may be other more sophisticated methods used in the collaborative filtering area that could provide even greater value. The paper could benefit from comparing against some of them.
    *   The reliance on LLM feedback for fine-tuning might introduce bias and could be computationally expensive. The paper could benefit from discussing potential solutions to this problem.
    *   The LaMP benchmark, while relevant, may not fully capture the complexity of real-world personalized text generation scenarios. More diverse and realistic evaluation is needed to assess the generalizability of the method.
    *   The performance gains relative to other personalized RAG methods aren't that significant, suggesting the degree of improvement and potential for impact might be limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence could be in:
    *   Inspiring further research on integrating collaborative filtering and other techniques from recommender systems into personalized RAG.
    *   Providing a framework for leveraging LLM feedback to improve retrieval models.
    *   Highlighting the importance of considering group knowledge in personalized text generation.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to personalized text generation, the incremental gains over prior work, combined with the weaknesses outlined above, limit its overall impact. The integration of collaborative filtering with RAG is a good idea, but the limited gains mean this isn't a huge breakthrough. The reliance on LLM feedback, while sensible, could also potentially introduce bias. A score of 6 reflects the work's decent novelty and significance, balanced by the identified weaknesses and limited impact, especially compared to the most revolutionary and high-impact publications.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM$\times$MapReduce-V2: Entropy-Driven Convolutional Test-Time Scaling for Generating Long-Form Articles from Extremely Long Resources
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05732v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyu Wang, Yujia Fu, Zhu Zhang, Shuo Wang, Zirui Ren, Xiaorong Wang, Zhili Li, Chaoqun He, Bo An, Zhiyuan Liu, Maosong Sun
- **Abstract**: Long-form generation is crucial for a wide range of practical applications, typically categorized into short-to-long and long-to-long generation. While short-to-long generations have received considerable attention, generating long texts from extremely long resources remains relatively underexplored. The primary challenge in long-to-long generation lies in effectively integrating and analyzing relevant information from extensive inputs, which remains difficult for current large language models (LLMs). In this paper, we propose LLM$\times$MapReduce-V2, a novel test-time scaling strategy designed to enhance the ability of LLMs to process extremely long inputs. Drawing inspiration from convolutional neural networks, which iteratively integrate local features into higher-level global representations, LLM$\times$MapReduce-V2 utilizes stacked convolutional scaling layers to progressively expand the understanding of input materials. Both quantitative and qualitative experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially enhances the ability of LLMs to process long inputs and generate coherent, informative long-form articles, outperforming several representative baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, based on the provided information:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLM×MapReduce-V2, a novel test-time scaling strategy designed to enhance large language models (LLMs) in processing extremely long inputs for long-form generation. It tackles the challenge of resource utilization, focusing on synthesizing information rather than simply extracting it.  Drawing inspiration from convolutional neural networks, LLM×MapReduce-V2 uses stacked convolutional scaling layers to progressively expand understanding.  The paper also introduces SurveyEval, a new high-quality survey writing benchmark for evaluation.  Experimental results on SurveyEval demonstrate significant improvements over baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and challenging problem in the field of long-form text generation: effectively utilizing extremely long input resources. The shift from extractive to integrative approaches is a valuable contribution, as it offers a potential solution to the limitations of existing methods that may overlook crucial contextual information. The theoretical analysis based on information bottleneck principles provides a solid foundation for the proposed framework.

The LLM×MapReduce-V2 framework itself appears innovative, drawing inspiration from convolutional neural networks for information aggregation.  The use of entropy-driven convolution and a Best-of-N self-refinement mechanism seems promising for enhancing skeleton quality and improving information content.  However, the practical implementation and effectiveness are heavily dependent on the specific parameters and architecture of the scaling layers, which require further scrutiny and justification.

A major strength is the introduction of SurveyEval, a new benchmark designed to evaluate long-to-long generation capabilities.  The inclusion of complete reference papers makes it a valuable resource for future research and enables a more thorough comparison of resource utilization capabilities.  The experimental results, demonstrating substantial improvements over baselines, provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of LLM×MapReduce-V2.

**Potential Weaknesses and Future Directions:**

*   **Limited Verification:** The paper is only verified on the survey task. The generalizability of LLM×MapReduce-V2 to other long-form generation tasks (e.g., research reports, creative writing) needs to be explored.
*   **Model Dependency:** The reliance on Gemini-2.0-flash-thinking-exp-1219 raises questions about the method's performance with more powerful and state-of-the-art models.  Testing with models like DeepSeek-R1 or others would strengthen the claims of the paper.
*   **Hallucination Mitigation:** As the paper itself admits, the hallucination issues of the base LLM can lead to errors. The framework doesn't seem to explicitly address this, a critical concern in generation tasks.
*   **Over-Optimization of Survey:** The survey generation itself may not be indicative of real world performance due to the structural nature.

**Overall Significance:** The paper presents a potentially significant advancement in long-form text generation by addressing the challenges of resource utilization. The LLM×MapReduce-V2 framework and the SurveyEval benchmark offer valuable contributions to the field.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents an innovative approach to a relevant problem with solid theoretical underpinnings and promising experimental results. However, the limited scope of verification, model dependency, and lack of hallucination mitigation warrant a cautious assessment. Further research is needed to fully establish the generalizability and robustness of the proposed framework.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rank-Then-Score: Enhancing Large Language Models for Automated Essay Scoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05736v1)
- **Authors**: Yida Cai, Kun Liang, Sanwoo Lee, Qinghan Wang, Yunfang Wu
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large language models (LLMs) achieve remarkable success across a variety of tasks. However, their potential in the domain of Automated Essay Scoring (AES) remains largely underexplored. Moreover, compared to English data, the methods for Chinese AES is not well developed. In this paper, we propose Rank-Then-Score (RTS), a fine-tuning framework based on large language models to enhance their essay scoring capabilities. Specifically, we fine-tune the ranking model (Ranker) with feature-enriched data, and then feed the output of the ranking model, in the form of a candidate score set, with the essay content into the scoring model (Scorer) to produce the final score. Experimental results on two benchmark datasets, HSK and ASAP, demonstrate that RTS consistently outperforms the direct prompting (Vanilla) method in terms of average QWK across all LLMs and datasets, and achieves the best performance on Chinese essay scoring using the HSK dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Rank-Then-Score (RTS), a novel two-stage fine-tuning framework for enhancing Large Language Models (LLMs) in the task of Automated Essay Scoring (AES), particularly for Chinese essays, where existing methods are underdeveloped. The RTS framework consists of two models: a Ranker, fine-tuned to rank essays based on pairwise comparisons and enriched with essay-related features, and a Scorer, fine-tuned to predict the final score based on the essay content and the candidate score set generated by the Ranker. The method uses a binary search tree approach to efficiently generate candidate scores. Experiments on HSK (Chinese) and ASAP (English) datasets demonstrate that RTS outperforms direct prompting (Vanilla) methods and achieves competitive performance compared to other strong baselines, especially on the HSK dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of techniques rather than groundbreaking individual components. The idea of incorporating ranking into the scoring process is not entirely new, but the RTS framework offers a unique approach:

    *   **Two-Stage Fine-Tuning:** Decoupling ranking and scoring into distinct fine-tuning stages tailored for LLMs is a good design choice, allowing the models to focus on specific sub-tasks that align well with LLM strengths.
    *   **Binary Search Tree for Candidate Scores:** This approach is interesting and offers a more structured way to leverage the ranking information compared to simply adding a ranking loss.
    *   **Focus on Chinese AES:** Addressing the relative lack of research on Chinese AES is a valuable contribution.

    However, none of these individual ideas are completely unprecedented. The novelty is in the *integration* of these techniques in a cohesive pipeline for improving AES performance with LLMs.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate:

    *   **Performance Improvement:** The experimental results show consistent outperformance of the Vanilla method and competitive results compared to existing approaches, particularly in the Chinese HSK dataset.
    *   **Insights on LLM Behavior:** The ablation studies and upper-bound analysis provide insights into the impact of features and the limitations of candidate score accuracy on the overall performance.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The method is relatively straightforward to implement and can potentially improve the accuracy and reliability of AES systems, especially for languages with limited existing resources.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear explanation of the RTS framework.
    *   Well-designed experiments with appropriate datasets and evaluation metrics.
    *   Detailed analysis of the results and ablation studies providing insights into the method's behavior.
    *   Addresses a gap in research on Chinese AES.
    *   The introduction of the Chinese HSK dataset could be useful for the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from stronger baselines and comparisons, particularly against more recent LLM-based AES methods.
    *   The improvement on ASAP dataset is somewhat modest, indicating that the approach might be more effective for Chinese essays or require further tuning for English.
    *   The limitations regarding reference essay selection could be further explored.
    *   The paper does not sufficiently account for the computational cost of deploying two separate LLMs. This is a practical consideration that should be addressed.
    *   While HSK dataset provides a useful resource for the Chinese language, the analysis of its characteristics is somewhat cursory.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM-based AES by:

    *   Encouraging the development of specialized fine-tuning strategies.
    *   Highlighting the importance of incorporating ranking information into the scoring process.
    *   Providing a useful framework for Chinese AES.
    *   Promoting the use of feature engineering and appropriate evaluation metrics in AES.
    *   Directing researchers towards more efficient methods for reference essay selection.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and useful approach to AES with LLMs.  It demonstrates tangible improvements over naive LLM usage, particularly in a less-explored area (Chinese AES). However, it doesn't revolutionize the field. The components, while cleverly integrated, aren't individually transformative. The results are solid, but the margin of improvement, particularly on the established ASAP dataset, suggests that there's room for further optimization and exploration of other possible strategies. The limitations regarding dataset bias and computational costs are also significant practical concerns. Therefore, a score reflecting a solid contribution with room for further improvement is appropriate.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### DDT: Decoupled Diffusion Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05741v1)
- **Authors**: Shuai Wang, Zhi Tian, Weilin Huang, Limin Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion transformers have demonstrated remarkable generation quality, albeit requiring longer training iterations and numerous inference steps. In each denoising step, diffusion transformers encode the noisy inputs to extract the lower-frequency semantic component and then decode the higher frequency with identical modules. This scheme creates an inherent optimization dilemma: encoding low-frequency semantics necessitates reducing high-frequency components, creating tension between semantic encoding and high-frequency decoding. To resolve this challenge, we propose a new \textbf{\color{ddt}D}ecoupled \textbf{\color{ddt}D}iffusion \textbf{\color{ddt}T}ransformer~(\textbf{\color{ddt}DDT}), with a decoupled design of a dedicated condition encoder for semantic extraction alongside a specialized velocity decoder. Our experiments reveal that a more substantial encoder yields performance improvements as model size increases. For ImageNet $256\times256$, Our DDT-XL/2 achieves a new state-of-the-art performance of {1.31 FID}~(nearly $4\times$ faster training convergence compared to previous diffusion transformers). For ImageNet $512\times512$, Our DDT-XL/2 achieves a new state-of-the-art FID of 1.28. Additionally, as a beneficial by-product, our decoupled architecture enhances inference speed by enabling the sharing self-condition between adjacent denoising steps. To minimize performance degradation, we propose a novel statistical dynamic programming approach to identify optimal sharing strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "DDT: Decoupled Diffusion Transformer" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Decoupled Diffusion Transformer (DDT) architecture for image generation, aiming to address the optimization dilemma in existing diffusion transformers. The core idea is to decouple the encoding of low-frequency semantic information from the decoding of high-frequency details.  DDT achieves this using a dedicated condition encoder to extract semantic self-conditions and a separate velocity decoder.  The authors claim that DDT enables faster training convergence and improved performance compared to existing diffusion transformer models.  They further propose a statistical dynamic programming approach for sharing self-condition information between adjacent denoising steps to enhance inference speed.  The paper presents results on ImageNet 256x256 and 512x512 datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art FID scores and improved training efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of decoupling the encoder and decoder in a diffusion transformer is relatively novel in the context of diffusion models. While encoder-decoder architectures are common in other areas of computer vision, their application to diffusion transformers to specifically address the optimization trade-off between semantic encoding and detail generation is a good contribution. The statistical dynamic programming approach for self-condition sharing is also a worthwhile addition that leads to faster convergence.

*   **Significance:** The reported results are significant. Achieving state-of-the-art FID scores on ImageNet, particularly with notably faster training, would have a substantial impact on the diffusion modeling community. The speedup in training is especially valuable, as it reduces the computational cost associated with large-scale diffusion model training. The demonstrated improvement could enable faster experimentation and development of new diffusion-based applications. Also, the speedup for inference makes diffusion models more practical in the real world.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly identifies the optimization dilemma in standard diffusion transformers.
    *   Well-motivated design: The decoupled architecture is logically motivated by the problem definition.
    *   Strong empirical results: The paper presents strong experimental results, demonstrating improved FID scores and training speed.
    *   Practical acceleration: The inference speedup via self-condition sharing is a valuable contribution.
    *   The ablation studies offer some limited insight to DDT's performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental Improvements: Several components such as, "Recent architectural improvements such as SwiGLU, ROPE, and RMSNorm have been ex-tensively validated in the research community", are incorporated from pre-existing methods. However, DDT appears to achieve a better result, so there's still good value.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a clear, well-motivated approach to a relevant problem in diffusion modeling. The DDT architecture and the self-condition sharing strategy represent a valuable contribution. The most appealing aspect of the paper is that both training and inference are sped up. While the techniques they use build upon existing works, the results are highly persuasive.

Therefore, a score of 8 is justified as a solid, original, and valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SEA-LION: Southeast Asian Languages in One Network
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05747v1)
- **Authors**: Raymond Ng, Thanh Ngan Nguyen, Yuli Huang, Ngee Chia Tai, Wai Yi Leong, Wei Qi Leong, Xianbin Yong, Jian Gang Ngui, Yosephine Susanto, Nicholas Cheng, Hamsawardhini Rengarajan, Peerat Limkonchotiwat, Adithya Venkatadri Hulagadri, Kok Wai Teng, Yeo Yeow Tong, Bryan Siow, Wei Yi Teo, Wayne Lau, Choon Meng Tan, Brandon Ong, Zhi Hao Ong, Jann Railey Montalan, Adwin Chan, Sajeban Antonyrex, Ren Lee, Esther Choa, David Ong Tat-Wee, Bing Jie Darius Liu, William Chandra Tjhi, Erik Cambria, Leslie Teo
- **Abstract**: Recently, Large Language Models (LLMs) have dominated much of the artificial intelligence scene with their ability to process and generate natural languages. However, the majority of LLM research and development remains English-centric, leaving low-resource languages such as those in the Southeast Asian (SEA) region under-represented. To address this representation gap, we introduce Llama-SEA-LION-v3-8B-IT and Gemma-SEA-LION-v3-9B-IT, two cutting-edge multilingual LLMs designed for SEA languages. The SEA-LION family of LLMs supports 11 SEA languages, namely English, Chinese, Indonesian, Vietnamese, Malay, Thai, Burmese, Lao, Filipino, Tamil, and Khmer. Our work leverages large-scale multilingual continued pre-training with a comprehensive post-training regime involving multiple stages of instruction fine-tuning, alignment, and model merging. Evaluation results on multilingual benchmarks indicate that our models achieve state-of-the-art performance across LLMs supporting SEA languages. We open-source the models to benefit the wider SEA community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SEA-LION paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEA-LION, a family of multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) specifically designed to improve performance on Southeast Asian (SEA) languages. The authors address the under-representation of SEA languages in existing English-centric LLMs.  They present two models, Llama-SEA-LION-v3-8B-IT and Gemma-SEA-LION-v3-9B-IT, built upon Llama 3 and Gemma 2, respectively. The models are trained using continued pre-training (CPT) and a comprehensive post-training regime involving instruction fine-tuning, alignment, and model merging.  The paper demonstrates that the SEA-LION models achieve state-of-the-art performance across SEA languages compared to other LLMs, while also maintaining competitive English language performance.  The models and training details are open-sourced.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Addressing an Important Gap:**  The paper directly tackles the critical issue of language representation bias in LLMs, focusing on the often-overlooked SEA region.  This is a significant contribution to making AI more inclusive and accessible globally.
    * **Comprehensive Training Methodology:** The paper details a comprehensive training process, including data curation, CPT with carefully selected data ratios, and a multi-stage post-training regime. This rigorous approach provides valuable insights for the community. The combination of instruction fine-tuning, alignment, and model merging is well-justified and shows thoughtful experimentation.
    * **Open-Source Release:** The release of both the models and training details promotes reproducibility and enables further research and development by others. This is a crucial factor in advancing the field and allowing researchers with limited resources to contribute.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper provides quantitative evaluations on SEA-HELM and Open LLM Leaderboard benchmarks, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance in SEA languages while maintaining competitive English performance. The performance comparison against other SEA-focused LLMs and general LLMs strengthens the paper's claims.
    * **Detailed Ablation Analysis:** The authors include a performance analysis to help understand the effect of their process.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Evaluation Scope:** While the SEA-HELM benchmark is used, the authors acknowledge the lack of proper benchmarks for low-resource SEA languages like Lao, Khmer, and Filipino. The evaluation focuses primarily on Indonesian, Tamil, Thai, and Vietnamese. Expanding the evaluation to include these less-represented languages would further strengthen the impact. It can be argued that because of that, the LLM hasn't "achieved state-of-the-art" performance across *all* SEA languages.
    * **Incremental Novelty:** The techniques used (CPT, instruction fine-tuning, model merging) are well-established in the field. While the application to SEA languages is novel, the methodology itself builds on existing approaches. The novelty lies more in the specific combination of techniques and the dataset curation than in groundbreaking algorithmic innovation.
    * **Limited Linguistic Diversity:** The study covers 11 SEA languages, it could have been more impactful by covering other rare SEA languages.
    * **Computational Cost:** The authors mention the significant GPU hours required for training. While they used Liger Kernel for memory savings, the high computational cost may limit accessibility for some researchers.

* **Significance:**

    * The SEA-LION models provide a strong baseline for future research and development in SEA language LLMs.
    * The detailed training methodology offers valuable insights for the community working on multilingual LLMs.
    * The open-source release encourages broader participation and innovation in the field.
    * The paper highlights the importance of addressing language representation bias and promoting inclusivity in AI.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper addresses a significant problem and presents strong empirical results, the novelty lies primarily in the application of existing techniques to a specific language group. The comprehensive training methodology and open-source release are valuable contributions, but the lack of groundbreaking algorithmic innovation and the limitations in evaluation scope prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. Specifically, a better benchmark, even a synthetically created one, to account for all SEA languages would drastically improve the paper.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Transferable Mask Transformer: Cross-domain Semantic Segmentation with Region-adaptive Transferability Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05774v1)
- **Authors**: Enming Zhang, Zhengyu Li, Yanru Wu, Jingge Wang, Yang Tan, Ruizhe Zhao, Guan Wang, Yang Li
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Vision Transformers (ViTs) have set new benchmarks in semantic segmentation. However, when adapting pretrained ViTs to new target domains, significant performance degradation often occurs due to distribution shifts, resulting in suboptimal global attention. Since self-attention mechanisms are inherently data-driven, they may fail to effectively attend to key objects when source and target domains exhibit differences in texture, scale, or object co-occurrence patterns. While global and patch-level domain adaptation methods provide partial solutions, region-level adaptation with dynamically shaped regions is crucial due to spatial heterogeneity in transferability across different image areas. We present Transferable Mask Transformer (TMT), a novel region-level adaptation framework for semantic segmentation that aligns cross-domain representations through spatial transferability analysis. TMT consists of two key components: (1) An Adaptive Cluster-based Transferability Estimator (ACTE) that dynamically segments images into structurally and semantically coherent regions for localized transferability assessment, and (2) A Transferable Masked Attention (TMA) module that integrates region-specific transferability maps into ViTs' attention mechanisms, prioritizing adaptation in regions with low transferability and high semantic uncertainty. Comprehensive evaluations across 20 cross-domain pairs demonstrate TMT's superiority, achieving an average 2% MIoU improvement over vanilla fine-tuning and a 1.28% increase compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The source code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Transferable Mask Transformer (TMT) to improve cross-domain semantic segmentation using Vision Transformers (ViTs). TMT tackles the problem of performance degradation when pre-trained ViTs are applied to new target domains due to distribution shifts. The core idea is to perform region-adaptive transferability estimation using two main components: 1) Adaptive Cluster-based Transferability Estimator (ACTE), which dynamically segments images into coherent regions for localized transferability assessment, and 2) Transferable Masked Attention (TMA), a module that incorporates region-specific transferability maps into ViTs' attention mechanisms, focusing adaptation on regions with low transferability and high semantic uncertainty. The experiments demonstrate that TMT outperforms vanilla fine-tuning and state-of-the-art baselines across multiple cross-domain datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its region-adaptive approach to transfer learning for ViTs. Unlike global or patch-level adaptation methods, TMT dynamically segments images into structurally and semantically coherent regions to assess transferability, which allows for a more fine-grained adaptation strategy. Combining this region-adaptive assessment with a masked attention mechanism that prioritizes adaptation in low-transferability regions further enhances the approach's novelty. The idea of learning region-specific transferability is interesting, and the use of proxy A-distance is a reasonable approach to measure transferability. The architecture combining ACTE and TMA is relatively novel.

*   **Significance:** The problem of distribution shifts in semantic segmentation is well-recognized, and this paper provides a plausible solution by focusing on region-adaptive transfer learning. The experimental results, showing a 2% MIoU improvement over vanilla fine-tuning and a 1.28% improvement over the SOTA, support the effectiveness of the proposed approach. This can contribute to more robust semantic segmentation models that generalize well to unseen domains, which has practical implications in autonomous driving and other applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated problem and clear explanation of the proposed approach.
    *   The region-adaptive transferability estimation is an interesting and potentially effective idea.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across various datasets and transfer pairs.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrate the importance of the ACTE and TMA components.
    *   Visualizations provide insights into the transferability maps and attention mechanisms.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The adaptive clustering method, while contributing to the overall novelty, might not be completely groundbreaking, as it is inspired by existing superpixel learning techniques.
    *   While the experimental results are positive, the improvements over the state-of-the-art, although statistically significant, are not dramatically large.  A deeper exploration of why the performance gains aren't even higher would strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational overhead introduced by the ACTE and TMA modules. Understanding the trade-off between performance and computational cost is crucial for practical applications.
    *   The impact of hyperparameters, particularly in the transferability-guided masked attention, needs to be justified. A more rigorous sensitivity analysis or adaptive hyperparameter strategy may be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in transfer learning for semantic segmentation, especially in ViT-based models. The region-adaptive approach could inspire new methods for addressing distribution shifts in other computer vision tasks.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach with promising results, addressing a significant challenge in semantic segmentation. However, the improvement over the state-of-the-art is relatively modest, and the proposed approach has weaknesses regarding computational overhead and hyperparameter tuning. Also, the adaptive clustering method leverages existing techniques. Despite these weaknesses, the region-adaptive transfer learning framework is a valuable contribution that could lead to further research in this area.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MDK12-Bench: A Multi-Discipline Benchmark for Evaluating Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05782v1)
- **Authors**: Pengfei Zhou, Fanrui Zhang, Xiaopeng Peng, Zhaopan Xu, Jiaxin Ai, Yansheng Qiu, Chuanhao Li, Zhen Li, Ming Li, Yukang Feng, Jianwen Sun, Haoquan Zhang, Zizhen Li, Xiaofeng Mao, Wangbo Zhao, Kai Wang, Xiaojun Chang, Wenqi Shao, Yang You, Kaipeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal reasoning, which integrates language and visual cues into problem solving and decision making, is a fundamental aspect of human intelligence and a crucial step toward artificial general intelligence. However, the evaluation of multimodal reasoning capabilities in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) remains inadequate. Most existing reasoning benchmarks are constrained by limited data size, narrow domain coverage, and unstructured knowledge distribution. To close these gaps, we introduce MDK12-Bench, a multi-disciplinary benchmark assessing the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs via real-world K-12 examinations. Spanning six disciplines (math, physics, chemistry, biology, geography, and information science), our benchmark comprises 140K reasoning instances across diverse difficulty levels from primary school to 12th grade. It features 6,827 instance-level knowledge point annotations based on a well-organized knowledge structure, detailed answer explanations, difficulty labels and cross-year partitions, providing a robust platform for comprehensive evaluation. Additionally, we present a novel dynamic evaluation framework to mitigate data contamination issues by bootstrapping question forms, question types, and image styles during evaluation. Extensive experiment on MDK12-Bench reveals the significant limitation of current MLLMs in multimodal reasoning. The findings on our benchmark provide insights into the development of the next-generation models. Our data and codes are available at https://github.com/LanceZPF/MDK12.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MDK12-Bench, a new multi-disciplinary benchmark designed to evaluate the reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) using real-world K-12 examination questions. The benchmark covers six disciplines (math, physics, chemistry, biology, geography, and information science) and includes 140K instances with fine-grained knowledge point annotations, detailed answer explanations, and difficulty labels.  Furthermore, the paper presents a dynamic evaluation framework that mitigates data contamination issues by bootstrapping question forms, types, and image styles during evaluation. Extensive experiments on MDK12-Bench show the limitations of current MLLMs in multimodal reasoning and provide insights for developing next-generation models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by introducing a large-scale, multi-disciplinary K-12 benchmark for evaluating MLLMs. While existing benchmarks exist, MDK12-Bench distinguishes itself through its breadth of disciplines, detailed knowledge structure annotations, and the incorporation of a dynamic evaluation framework. The dynamic evaluation, while not entirely new in concept, is applied in a novel way within the context of a K-12 benchmark to address data contamination, which is a significant problem in the field.  The level of granularity in the annotations (instance-level knowledge points linked to a knowledge tree) is a strength that enables more fine-grained error analysis.

* **Significance:** The K-12 domain is well-suited for evaluating fundamental reasoning skills across a variety of subjects, mimicking a crucial stage in human cognitive development. By leveraging real-world examination questions, the benchmark assesses the model's ability to solve practical, knowledge-intensive problems. The inclusion of a dynamic evaluation framework enhances the benchmark's reliability and fairness, which are critical for accurately comparing different models.  The analysis of MLLM performance on the benchmark sheds light on the weaknesses of current models and highlights specific areas (e.g., contextual understanding, resistance to perturbation) that require further research. This benchmark can accelerate the improvement of multi-modal reasoning capabilities.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Large Scale and Multi-Disciplinarity:**  The size and scope of MDK12-Bench make it a more challenging and comprehensive evaluation resource than many existing benchmarks.
    *   **Structured Knowledge Representation:** The detailed annotations and knowledge structure provide a strong foundation for in-depth analysis of model performance.
    *   **Dynamic Evaluation:** The bootstrapping approach helps mitigate data contamination and ensures the benchmark remains relevant as models evolve.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The focus on K-12 examination questions provides a concrete and well-defined problem space for evaluating reasoning skills.
    *   **Comprehensive Leaderboard:**  The extensive experiments and detailed analysis on various MLLMs provides valuable insights and a strong baseline for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * The description of the bootstrapping strategies could be improved with more concrete examples.
    * The reliance on GPT to judge the answer matching and adaptation has its limitations, as even advanced LLMs are not perfect judges of nuanced reasoning.
    *  The paper states that Gemini2 thinking excels in biology and chemistry, but the paper does not define the specifics that makes them perform well in these areas.
    * The improvement of models could have been enhanced by using the data generated from the study to create a model of their own that excelled in these tests.

* **Potential Influence:** MDK12-Bench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating and advancing MLLMs in the field of AI. The benchmark's structure and the dynamic evaluation framework offer valuable tools for researchers to develop more robust and reliable models. By focusing on fundamental reasoning skills across a range of disciplines, MDK12-Bench can contribute to progress towards more general-purpose AI. The insights gained from using this benchmark can guide future research in areas such as knowledge representation, reasoning algorithms, and multi-modal integration.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** MDK12-Bench is a significant contribution to the MLLM evaluation landscape. It offers a substantial improvement in terms of scale, coverage, annotation quality, and data contamination mitigation compared to many existing benchmarks. While some aspects of the methodology, especially the reliance on LLMs for answer judgment, could be further refined, the overall impact of the paper is substantial. The benchmark's potential to drive research and development in the field of MLLMs, as well as its innovative dynamic evaluation framework, warrant a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### How to Enable LLM with 3D Capacity? A Survey of Spatial Reasoning in LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05786v1)
- **Authors**: Jirong Zha, Yuxuan Fan, Xiao Yang, Chen Gao, Xinlei Chen
- **Abstract**: 3D spatial understanding is essential in real-world applications such as robotics, autonomous vehicles, virtual reality, and medical imaging. Recently, Large Language Models (LLMs), having demonstrated remarkable success across various domains, have been leveraged to enhance 3D understanding tasks, showing potential to surpass traditional computer vision methods. In this survey, we present a comprehensive review of methods integrating LLMs with 3D spatial understanding. We propose a taxonomy that categorizes existing methods into three branches: image-based methods deriving 3D understanding from 2D visual data, point cloud-based methods working directly with 3D representations, and hybrid modality-based methods combining multiple data streams. We systematically review representative methods along these categories, covering data representations, architectural modifications, and training strategies that bridge textual and 3D modalities. Finally, we discuss current limitations, including dataset scarcity and computational challenges, while highlighting promising research directions in spatial perception, multi-modal fusion, and real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided survey paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of methods that integrate Large Language Models (LLMs) with 3D spatial understanding capabilities. It categorizes existing approaches into three main branches: image-based, point cloud-based, and hybrid modality-based methods. The survey reviews representative methods within each category, discussing data representations, architectural modifications, and training strategies. The authors also address current limitations like data scarcity and computational challenges, highlighting promising research directions in spatial perception, multi-modal fusion, and real-world applications. The paper aims to provide a comprehensive overview and identify future research areas in this rapidly developing field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While surveys in rapidly evolving fields are valuable, the novelty here is limited.  The authors acknowledge that a previous survey (Ma et al., 2024b) already covers the field. They argue that their survey is necessary because of recent developments, but the extent to which they present novel insights or perspectives *distinct from Ma et al.* is questionable. The proposed taxonomy is a slight refinement, but the core categories are inherently intuitive. The paper would benefit from a deeper critical analysis of the existing literature, rather than a mostly descriptive summary.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The field of LLMs and 3D understanding is indeed burgeoning, and an up-to-date overview is useful for researchers entering the area. By highlighting limitations (data scarcity, computational costs, weak spatial reasoning) and future directions (enhanced 3D perception, multi-modal fusion, application to autonomous systems), the survey may indirectly influence research agendas. The breakdown of the different data representations and alignment strategies also provides a helpful reference for practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper covers a reasonably broad range of recent works, particularly given the field's rapid development.
    *   **Clear Categorization:** The proposed taxonomy, while not revolutionary, provides a structured way to understand the different approaches.
    *   **Identification of Key Challenges:** The paper clearly articulates the main limitations that need to be addressed for further progress.
    *   **Future directions:**  The overview of future research areas is helpful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Critical Analysis:** The survey is primarily descriptive, lacking a deeper critical analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.  The authors don't delve into *why* certain methods are more successful than others or how the field might overcome fundamental limitations.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The novelty beyond the previously published survey (Ma et al., 2024b) is not significant enough to justify a very high score.
    *   **Lack of Depth:** While covering a broad range of papers, the analysis of each paper is relatively shallow. The survey would be more impactful if it focused on fewer papers but provided a more in-depth analysis.
    *   **Missing Context:** A slightly deeper discussion of the *underlying causes* of the "weak spatial reasoning" challenge would be beneficial. Is it fundamentally a limitation of LLM architecture, data representation, or training methodology?

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper provides a useful, up-to-date overview of a rapidly developing field. However, the limited novelty and shallow critical analysis prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The primary value lies in its categorization of existing methods and identification of challenges, but the depth of analysis and unique insights are not substantial enough for a high score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Storybooth: Training-free Multi-Subject Consistency for Improved Visual Storytelling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05800v1)
- **Authors**: Jaskirat Singh, Junshen Kevin Chen, Jonas Kohler, Michael Cohen
- **Abstract**: Training-free consistent text-to-image generation depicting the same subjects across different images is a topic of widespread recent interest. Existing works in this direction predominantly rely on cross-frame self-attention; which improves subject-consistency by allowing tokens in each frame to pay attention to tokens in other frames during self-attention computation. While useful for single subjects, we find that it struggles when scaling to multiple characters. In this work, we first analyze the reason for these limitations. Our exploration reveals that the primary-issue stems from self-attention-leakage, which is exacerbated when trying to ensure consistency across multiple-characters. This happens when tokens from one subject pay attention to other characters, causing them to appear like each other (e.g., a dog appearing like a duck). Motivated by these findings, we propose StoryBooth: a training-free approach for improving multi-character consistency. In particular, we first leverage multi-modal chain-of-thought reasoning and region-based generation to apriori localize the different subjects across the desired story outputs. The final outputs are then generated using a modified diffusion model which consists of two novel layers: 1) a bounded cross-frame self-attention layer for reducing inter-character attention leakage, and 2) token-merging layer for improving consistency of fine-grain subject details. Through both qualitative and quantitative results we find that the proposed approach surpasses prior state-of-the-art, exhibiting improved consistency across both multiple-characters and fine-grain subject details.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the StoryBooth paper:

**Summary:**

The StoryBooth paper addresses the challenge of generating consistent characters across multiple images in a storyboard setting. It identifies a limitation of existing training-free approaches that rely on cross-frame self-attention: *self-attention leakage*, where tokens from one character influence the appearance of other characters, especially when generating scenes with multiple subjects. To mitigate this, StoryBooth proposes a training-free approach that combines:

1.  **Region-based Storyboard Planning:**  Leveraging large language models (LLMs) for chain-of-thought reasoning to plan the storyboard layout and localize character regions *a priori*.
2.  **Bounded Cross-Frame Self-Attention:** A modified self-attention mechanism that restricts tokens to attend primarily to tokens from the same character across different frames, reducing leakage.
3.  **Cross-Frame Token Merging:** A technique to improve fine-grained consistency by merging low-level feature tokens across frames based on the attention map.
4. **Early Negative Token Unmerging:** Introduce the addition negative token mergin in earlier diffusion timesteps to produce pose variations to ensure that the output remains diverse.

The authors demonstrate, through qualitative and quantitative experiments, that StoryBooth improves character consistency, text-image alignment, and offers faster inference than optimization-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The identification and articulation of the *self-attention leakage* problem in multi-character consistent generation is a key contribution. While self-attention leakage is a known issue in other contexts, this paper highlights its specific challenges in storyboard generation.
    *   The combination of LLM-based region planning with a bounded cross-frame self-attention mechanism is a novel way to address the leakage problem.  The token merging technique further refines the consistency.
    *   However, the individual components (region-based generation, token merging) are not entirely new. The paper's novelty lies in the specific *combination* and adaptation of these components to address the identified problem.  Early negative token unmerging also adds on the novelty of this work.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The problem of consistent character generation is important for visual storytelling and content creation.  The StoryBooth approach offers a practical, training-free solution.
    *   The improvement in inference time compared to optimization-based methods is significant, making the approach more accessible for real-world applications.
    *   The paper's results demonstrate a clear improvement in character consistency and text-image alignment compared to existing training-free methods.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation, including qualitative results, quantitative metrics, and user studies.
    *   The proposed approach is practical and easy to implement.
    *   Significant performance gain in inference time compared to SoTA.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on LLM-based region planning introduces a dependency on the quality of the LLM's output. If the LLM struggles to generate accurate layouts, it could negatively impact the performance of StoryBooth.
    *   The computational cost of the LLM-based planning stage isn't explicitly discussed.  While the overall inference time is improved, the planning stage might add overhead.
    *   While training-free, the approach still requires careful tuning of parameters (e.g., dropout probability in self-attention bounding, token merging weights).
*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   StoryBooth could become a baseline for training-free consistent storyboard generation.
    *   The analysis of self-attention leakage could inspire further research into more robust attention mechanisms for multi-subject generation.
    *   The combination of LLM-based planning and constrained diffusion models could be applied to other image generation tasks requiring fine-grained control.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and effective solution to a relevant problem in image generation. The combination of LLM-based region planning with a novel bounded self-attention mechanism is a significant contribution.  The improvements in character consistency, text-image alignment, and inference time are substantial. While the individual components aren't entirely novel, the way they are combined and adapted to address self-attention leakage makes this a valuable contribution. I find the inclusion of negative token-unmerging to be a good insight, although it is not novel.
Therefore, a score of 7 is assigned, because the paper's insights on self-attention leakage and the system's modular approach can facilitate and improve future works in the direction.
*Score: 7*

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### StealthRank: LLM Ranking Manipulation via Stealthy Prompt Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05804v1)
- **Authors**: Yiming Tang, Yi Fan, Chenxiao Yu, Tiankai Yang, Yue Zhao, Xiyang Hu
- **Abstract**: The integration of large language models (LLMs) into information retrieval systems introduces new attack surfaces, particularly for adversarial ranking manipulations. We present StealthRank, a novel adversarial ranking attack that manipulates LLM-driven product recommendation systems while maintaining textual fluency and stealth. Unlike existing methods that often introduce detectable anomalies, StealthRank employs an energy-based optimization framework combined with Langevin dynamics to generate StealthRank Prompts (SRPs)-adversarial text sequences embedded within product descriptions that subtly yet effectively influence LLM ranking mechanisms. We evaluate StealthRank across multiple LLMs, demonstrating its ability to covertly boost the ranking of target products while avoiding explicit manipulation traces that can be easily detected. Our results show that StealthRank consistently outperforms state-of-the-art adversarial ranking baselines in both effectiveness and stealth, highlighting critical vulnerabilities in LLM-driven recommendation systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "StealthRank: LLM Ranking Manipulation via Stealthy Prompt Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces StealthRank, a novel adversarial ranking attack designed to manipulate LLM-driven product recommendation systems. Unlike existing techniques that often introduce detectable anomalies through perplexity or keyword use, StealthRank leverages an energy-based optimization framework combined with Langevin dynamics to generate "StealthRank Prompts" (SRPs).  These SRPs are subtly embedded within product descriptions, effectively influencing LLM ranking mechanisms while preserving textual fluency, contextual coherence, and avoiding overt promotional cues. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of StealthRank across multiple commercial LLMs (Llama-3.1-8B, Vicuna-7B, Mistral-7B, and DeepSeek-7B), showcasing superior performance in both rank elevation and stealth compared to state-of-the-art adversarial ranking baselines.  The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on generating highly stealthy adversarial prompts for LLM-based ranking manipulation. While adversarial attacks on LLMs are not new, the emphasis on maintaining fluency, coherence, and avoiding trigger words through energy-based optimization and Langevin dynamics is a significant advancement. Previous attacks often relied on injecting random or unnatural text, making them easier to detect. StealthRank's approach aims to bypass such detection mechanisms, representing a more sophisticated attack strategy. The technique of using Langevin dynamics in logit space for refining the prompt is clever, enabling subtle but effective manipulation.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical vulnerability in LLM-driven recommendation systems. As LLMs are increasingly integrated into information retrieval and e-commerce platforms, the risk of adversarial manipulation becomes a major concern. StealthRank demonstrates that even seemingly benign text insertions can significantly influence ranking outcomes. This has direct implications for fairness, security, and the integrity of online marketplaces. The fact that the code is publicly available will likely spur further research and the development of defensive strategies. It will also raise awareness among LLM developers and platform providers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of stealthy ranking manipulation and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Novel Approach:** StealthRank is a technically sound and innovative approach, effectively combining energy-based optimization and Langevin dynamics for prompt generation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are extensive, covering multiple LLMs and datasets.  The inclusion of human evaluation strengthens the argument for StealthRank's stealthiness.
    *   **Clear Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with a clear explanation of the methodology and results.
    *   **Publicly Available Code:** This fosters reproducibility and facilitates future research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset:** While the evaluation covers several LLMs and uses a dataset that is commonly used by this area of research, expanding the evaluation to include more diverse product categories and larger candidate sets would strengthen the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Limited Defense Evaluation:** The paper discusses potential defenses, but does not include rigorous evaluation or implementation of defense mechanisms to show real world impact.
    *   **Prompt Length:** The framework uses a fixed-length prompt that's appended to the product description. Adapting to variable-length and dynamic text would be a more significant challenge and increase the practical relevance of the findings.
    *   **Evaluation metric** The paper lacks a robust stealth evaluation metric other than Word Ratio, which relies on manual selection of words and could be improved by more recent measures.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of more robust and secure LLM-based ranking systems. It highlights the need for developers to consider adversarial vulnerabilities and implement effective defense strategies. Future research may focus on developing detection mechanisms for stealthy prompts and incorporating adversarial training techniques to improve LLM robustness.

**Justification for Score:**

While adversarial attacks on LLMs are an existing area of research, StealthRank's focus on stealth through a novel optimization approach and its demonstration of effectiveness across multiple LLMs represent a significant step forward. It provides a more sophisticated attack strategy than previous methods and underscores a critical vulnerability in LLM-driven recommendation systems. The public release of the code will undoubtedly stimulate further research in this area. Taking into account the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### PaMi-VDPO: Mitigating Video Hallucinations by Prompt-Aware Multi-Instance Video Preference Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05810v1)
- **Authors**: Xinpeng Ding, Kui Zhang, Jinahua Han, Lanqing Hong, Hang Xu, Xiaomeng Li
- **Abstract**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) helps reduce hallucinations in Video Multimodal Large Language Models (VLLMs), but its reliance on offline preference data limits adaptability and fails to capture true video-response misalignment. We propose Video Direct Preference Optimization (VDPO), an online preference learning framework that eliminates the need for preference annotation by leveraging video augmentations to generate rejected samples while keeping responses fixed. However, selecting effective augmentations is non-trivial, as some clips may be semantically identical to the original under specific prompts, leading to false rejections and disrupting alignment. To address this, we introduce Prompt-aware Multi-instance Learning VDPO (PaMi-VDPO), which selects augmentations based on prompt context. Instead of a single rejection, we construct a candidate set of augmented clips and apply a close-to-far selection strategy, initially ensuring all clips are semantically relevant while then prioritizing the most prompt-aware distinct clip. This allows the model to better capture meaningful visual differences, mitigating hallucinations, while avoiding false rejections, and improving alignment. PaMi-VDPOseamlessly integrates into existing VLLMs without additional parameters, GPT-4/human supervision. With only 10k SFT data, it improves the base model by 5.3% on VideoHallucer, surpassing GPT-4o, while maintaining stable performance on general video benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PaMi-VDPO: Mitigating Video Hallucinations by Prompt-Aware Multi-Instance Video Preference Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of video hallucinations in Video Multimodal Large Language Models (VLLMs). It proposes a new framework called Prompt-aware Multi-instance Learning Video DPO (PaMi-VDPO) to mitigate these hallucinations. Unlike existing Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) methods, PaMi-VDPO performs online preference learning by leveraging video augmentations to generate rejected samples while keeping responses fixed, thereby avoiding the need for manual preference annotations. The key innovation is to construct a candidate set of augmented clips and select the most prompt-aware and distinct one through a close-to-far selection strategy, preventing false rejections and improving alignment between video content and generated responses. The authors demonstrate that PaMi-VDPO improves performance on video hallucination benchmarks without additional parameters or architectural changes, while maintaining stable general performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to address video hallucinations in VLLMs, differing from standard DPO which relies on human/LLM-annotated preferences. The online video augmentation and the prompt-aware multi-instance learning framework are innovative contributions. The core idea of selecting augmentations that are semantically similar yet prompt-aware is well reasoned.

*   **Technical Soundness:** The proposed PaMi-VDPO framework is technically sound.  The close-to-far selection strategy is well-defined, and the use of Jensen-Shannon divergence for measuring output divergence provides a justifiable approach.  The equations are clear and the implementation details are reasonably described.

*   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper includes comprehensive experiments and ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of PaMi-VDPO. The authors compare their method against state-of-the-art VLLMs and demonstrate significant performance improvements on hallucination benchmarks. The ablation studies systematically investigate the impact of different components, providing insights into the framework's behavior. The evaluation utilizes established benchmarks and metrics.

*   **Practical Significance:**  PaMi-VDPO is practical as it seamlessly integrates into existing VLLMs without requiring architectural modifications or pre-constructed preference data, making it efficient and scalable. The demonstrated improvements, particularly the surpassing of GPT-4o on a hallucination benchmark using only 10K SFT data, make the method quite relevant.

*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly structured. The problem is well-motivated, the proposed method is explained in detail, and the experimental results are presented clearly with insightful discussions. The figures are helpful in visualizing the key concepts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Diversity of Augmentations:** While the augmentations explored cover basic temporal and visual aspects (cropping, shuffling, reversing etc.), the method might benefit from exploring more sophisticated or semantically richer augmentations. It is unclear if the proposed algorithm is limited by the specific set of augmentations studied.

*   **Dependency on LLM for "Prompt Awareness":** The "prompt awareness" hinges on the LLM's (i.e., LLAVA-OV-7B) understanding and responses to different augmentations. While JS divergence is used, relying on the LLM's output might inherit its own biases or hallucination tendencies, potentially affecting the selection of the best augmentation. This dependency, and its potential effects, could be further investigated.

*   **Potential for Overfitting:** The method focuses on the video segment for preference learning. This might potentially lead to overfitting, and therefore, further studies should be done on whether PaMi-VDPO would be able to generalize to unseen scenarios where the dataset characteristics deviate.

*   **Limited analysis into failure cases**: The qualitative analysis could have been improved by showing examples where the proposed method fails and explaining the potential reasons.

**Significance:**

This paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of video understanding and multimodal learning. The PaMi-VDPO framework offers a promising approach for mitigating video hallucinations and improving the reliability of VLLMs. The online preference learning strategy and prompt-aware multi-instance learning formulation have the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**.  PaMi-VDPO presents a novel, technically sound, and empirically validated approach to addressing video hallucinations, which is a crucial problem in the field. The avoidance of manual/LLM annotation for creating preference data is a significant strength, improving efficiency and adaptability. While the reliance on an LLM for "prompt awareness" introduces a dependency and the limited diversity of augmentations are weaknesses, the overall contribution is substantial.  The method's ability to outperform GPT-4o (according to the paper's claims) on a key benchmark is particularly noteworthy and signifies that the algorithm addresses a key deficiency in current vision-language models. The contribution should drive future exploration in this area.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Right Question is Already Half the Answer: Fully Unsupervised LLM Reasoning Incentivization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05812v1)
- **Authors**: Qingyang Zhang, Haitao Wu, Changqing Zhang, Peilin Zhao, Yatao Bian
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional capabilities in challenging tasks such as mathematical reasoning, existing methods to enhance reasoning ability predominantly rely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) on reasoning-specific data after pre-training. However, these approaches critically depend on external supervisions--such as human labelled reasoning traces, verified golden answers, or pre-trained reward models--which limits scalability and practical applicability. In this work, we propose Entropy Minimized Policy Optimization (EMPO), which makes an early attempt at fully unsupervised LLM reasoning incentivization. EMPO does not require any supervised information for incentivizing reasoning capabilities (i.e., neither verifiable reasoning traces, problems with golden answers, nor additional pre-trained reward models). By continuously minimizing the predictive entropy of LLMs on unlabeled user queries in a latent semantic space, EMPO enables purely self-supervised evolution of reasoning capabilities with strong flexibility and practicality. Our experiments demonstrate competitive performance of EMPO on both mathematical reasoning and free-form commonsense reasoning tasks. Specifically, without any supervised signals, EMPO boosts the accuracy of Qwen2.5-Math-7B Base from 30.7\% to 48.1\% on mathematical benchmarks and improves truthfulness accuracy of Qwen2.5-7B Instruct from 87.16\% to 97.25\% on TruthfulQA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Entropy Minimized Policy Optimization (EMPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm for incentivizing reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs) in a fully unsupervised manner. Unlike existing methods that rely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and/or reinforcement learning with human-labeled data or pre-trained reward models, EMPO requires no external supervision. It works by minimizing the predictive entropy of LLMs on unlabeled user queries in a latent semantic space, thereby encouraging the model to produce consistent and reliable answers.
